I think its potential influence in software engineering and legacy systems can be substantial. The combination of techniques and the explicit focus on practicality makes it a high-quality contribution to the field.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### FAITH: A Framework for Assessing Intrinsic Tabular Hallucinations in finance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05201v1)
- **Authors**: Mengao Zhang, Jiayu Fu, Tanya Warrier, Yuwen Wang, Tianhui Tan, Ke-wei Huang
- **Abstract**: Hallucination remains a critical challenge for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in finance. Accurate extraction and precise calculation from tabular data are essential for reliable financial analysis, since even minor numerical errors can undermine decision-making and regulatory compliance. Financial applications have unique requirements, often relying on context-dependent, numerical, and proprietary tabular data that existing hallucination benchmarks rarely capture. In this study, we develop a rigorous and scalable framework for evaluating intrinsic hallucinations in financial LLMs, conceptualized as a context-aware masked span prediction task over real-world financial documents. Our main contributions are: (1) a novel, automated dataset creation paradigm using a masking strategy; (2) a new hallucination evaluation dataset derived from S&P 500 annual reports; and (3) a comprehensive evaluation of intrinsic hallucination patterns in state-of-the-art LLMs on financial tabular data. Our work provides a robust methodology for in-house LLM evaluation and serves as a critical step toward building more trustworthy and reliable financial Generative AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "FAITH: A Framework for Assessing Intrinsic Tabular Hallucinations in Finance" addresses the critical challenge of hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) when applied to financial data. The authors propose a novel framework for evaluating intrinsic hallucinations in financial LLMs, focusing on context-aware masked span prediction using real-world financial documents. The main contributions include (1) an automated dataset creation paradigm using a masking strategy, (2) a new hallucination evaluation dataset derived from S&P 500 annual reports, and (3) a comprehensive evaluation of intrinsic hallucination patterns in state-of-the-art LLMs on financial tabular data. The framework is designed to be scalable and robust, addressing the limitations of existing hallucination benchmarks that often fail to capture the unique requirements of financial applications, such as context-dependency, numerical precision, and proprietary data. The paper also categorizes financial reasoning into four types (Direct Lookup, Comparative, Bivariate, and Multivariate Calculation) to enable structured evaluation across reasoning complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a significant step forward in the area of hallucination evaluation, specifically tailored to the financial domain. While the concept of masked span prediction is not entirely new, the application of this technique to the unique challenges of financial tabular data, the automated dataset creation paradigm, and the detailed taxonomy of financial reasoning scenarios demonstrate clear novelty. The work directly tackles the limitations of existing hallucination benchmarks which are often generalized to general-domain content and may not accurately capture the intricacies involved in financial analysis. The paper's innovation lies in the focus on intrinsic hallucinations within the complex context of financial tabular data, where precise numerical extraction and contextual understanding are vital. The study builds upon recent advancements in automated benchmarking but extends them to address the distinctive demands of financial tabular analysis.

*   **Significance:** Hallucinations pose a serious threat to the deployment of LLMs in finance, where even minor errors can have significant consequences. The proposed framework provides a valuable tool for evaluating and mitigating these risks, contributing to the development of more trustworthy and reliable financial AI systems. The research addresses a crucial gap in the field by offering a rigorous and scalable methodology for evaluating intrinsic hallucinations in financial LLMs. By providing a financial dataset, the paper paves the way for more targeted research on hallucination mitigation. The work is highly relevant given the increasing regulatory scrutiny of AI applications in finance and the need for robust model risk management frameworks. The classification of reasoning types allows for a structured approach to evaluation, aiding in identifying specific weaknesses of LLMs in different financial tasks. The detailed analysis of model performance and error patterns offers actionable guidance for researchers and practitioners. The study emphasizes the importance of high-fidelity numeric data and contextual grounding in financial applications. The framework could be useful for financial institutions performing internal LLM evaluations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Domain-Specific Focus:** The research is focused on a highly relevant and high-stakes domain, addressing a critical need for reliable LLMs in finance.
    *   **Scalable Methodology:** The automated dataset creation paradigm enables the construction of large-scale hallucination evaluation datasets for both public and proprietary financial documents.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study conducts a comprehensive analysis of intrinsic hallucination patterns in state-of-the-art LLMs, including a detailed breakdown by reasoning type.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings provide actionable guidance for researchers and practitioners, contributing to the development of more trustworthy and reliable financial AI systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Annotation:** While the authors demonstrate the reliability of LLMs for answerability annotation, the potential for biases introduced by LLMs remains a concern.
    *   **Limited Scope of Financial Tasks:** The study focuses primarily on tabular data and numeric claims, potentially overlooking other important financial tasks, such as textual analysis and risk assessment.
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the dataset covers a range of industries, it is limited to S&P 500 companies, potentially overlooking the unique characteristics of smaller or private financial institutions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by providing a standardized framework for evaluating hallucinations in financial LLMs. It is likely to spur further research on hallucination mitigation techniques and the development of more robust financial AI systems. The framework is crucial for the creation of future benchmarks.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above, I assign this paper a **Score: 8**.

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of financial AI by addressing the critical challenge of hallucinations in LLMs. The proposed framework is well-designed, scalable, and offers practical implications for researchers and practitioners. While there are some weaknesses, such as the reliance on LLMs for annotation and the limited scope of financial tasks, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The study addresses a crucial gap in the existing literature by specifically targeting the financial domain. The work also offers detailed findings and actional recommendations. The potential influence of the paper on the field is high, as it provides a standardized framework for hallucination evaluation and is likely to stimulate further research in this area. However, the score is not higher because the work lacks some of the comprehensive and long-lasting effect on the field that more paradigm-shifting papers possess.


- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SPEX: A Vision-Language Model for Land Cover Extraction on Spectral Remote Sensing Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05202v1)
- **Authors**: Dongchen Si, Di Wang, Erzhong Gao, Xiaolei Qin, Liu Zhao, Jing Zhang, Minqiang Xu, Jianbo Zhan, Jianshe Wang, Lin Liu, Bo Du, Liangpei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Spectral information has long been recognized as a critical cue in remote sensing observations. Although numerous vision-language models have been developed for pixel-level interpretation, spectral information remains underutilized, resulting in suboptimal performance, particularly in multispectral scenarios. To address this limitation, we construct a vision-language instruction-following dataset named SPIE, which encodes spectral priors of land-cover objects into textual attributes recognizable by large language models (LLMs), based on classical spectral index computations. Leveraging this dataset, we propose SPEX, a multimodal LLM designed for instruction-driven land cover extraction. To this end, we introduce several carefully designed components and training strategies, including multiscale feature aggregation, token context condensation, and multispectral visual pre-training, to achieve precise and flexible pixel-level interpretation. To the best of our knowledge, SPEX is the first multimodal vision-language model dedicated to land cover extraction in spectral remote sensing imagery. Extensive experiments on five public multispectral datasets demonstrate that SPEX consistently outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in extracting typical land cover categories such as vegetation, buildings, and water bodies. Moreover, SPEX is capable of generating textual explanations for its predictions, thereby enhancing interpretability and user-friendliness. Code will be released at: https://github.com/MiliLab/SPEX.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SPEX paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPEX, a novel vision-language model (VLM) specifically designed for instruction-driven land cover extraction from spectral remote sensing images. Recognizing the underutilization of spectral information in existing VLMs for remote sensing, the authors create the SPIE dataset, which encodes spectral properties of land cover objects into textual attributes using classical spectral index computations.  SPEX utilizes a multimodal LLM architecture with components such as multiscale feature aggregation, token context condensation, and multispectral visual pre-training to achieve accurate and flexible pixel-level interpretation.  Experiments on five public multispectral datasets demonstrate SPEX's superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods in extracting land cover categories and its ability to generate textual explanations for its predictions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel contribution by explicitly addressing the need to incorporate spectral information in VLMs for remote sensing. Creating a spectral-aware vision-language model by leveraging large language models alongside existing spectral indices is a good idea. While other remote sensing VLMs exist, they have not focused explicitly on using spectral characteristics in instruction based land cover extraction.

*   **Significance:** Land cover extraction is a foundational task in remote sensing, and the ability to perform this task with instruction-following and explainability enhances the practical usability of remote sensing data for non-experts. SPEX has the potential to improve land use planning, environmental monitoring, and other applications by providing more accurate and interpretable results. It also opens directions for more advanced models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a gap:**  The paper clearly identifies and addresses a crucial limitation of existing RSVLMs—their underutilization of spectral information.
    *   **Dataset construction:** The creation of the SPIE dataset is a valuable contribution, providing a resource for training and evaluating spectral-aware VLMs. Transforming multispectral images into vision-language pairs with textual attributes using classical indices enables the LLM to better understand remote sensing data.
    *   **Model architecture:**  The careful design of SPEX's architecture, with components like multiscale feature aggregation and token context condensation, addresses the specific challenges of processing spectral remote sensing data.
    *   **Experimental validation:**  The extensive experiments on multiple datasets provide strong evidence of SPEX's superior performance compared to existing methods.
    *   **Explainability:** The ability to generate textual explanations for its predictions enhances the interpretability and user-friendliness of SPEX.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Spectral Indices:** The core novelty stems from leveraging existing spectral indices. While effective, this limits the model's ability to learn complex relationships between spectral bands that go beyond what these indices capture.
    *   **Limited Land Cover Types:** Focus on vegetation, water and buildings is a little narrow.
    *   **Complexity:** The model has several components, like TCP and MSAM which adds to complexity.
    *   **Limited scope of instruction following:** The instructions are template driven and the modifications that are incorporated are spectral, this has some limitations regarding more varied instructions

*   **Impact:** The paper is well-written and presents a compelling solution to an important problem in remote sensing. SPEX represents a significant step towards more intelligent and user-friendly remote sensing systems. The SPIE dataset and the SPEX model can serve as a foundation for future research in spectral-aware VLMs for remote sensing.

**Justification for Score:**

While SPEX presents a valuable contribution by addressing a gap in existing RSVLMs, the method's reliance on existing spectral indices and somewhat limited instruction variety, combined with architectural complexity, holds it back from receiving a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper presents a clear advancement and offers a good baseline model with good performance metrics. Thus, it earns a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReasoningTrack: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Long-term Vision-Language Tracking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05221v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Wang, Liye Jin, Xufeng Lou, Shiao Wang, Lan Chen, Bo Jiang, Zhipeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Vision-language tracking has received increasing attention in recent years, as textual information can effectively address the inflexibility and inaccuracy associated with specifying the target object to be tracked. Existing works either directly fuse the fixed language with vision features or simply modify using attention, however, their performance is still limited. Recently, some researchers have explored using text generation to adapt to the variations in the target during tracking, however, these works fail to provide insights into the model's reasoning process and do not fully leverage the advantages of large models, which further limits their overall performance. To address the aforementioned issues, this paper proposes a novel reasoning-based vision-language tracking framework, named ReasoningTrack, based on a pre-trained vision-language model Qwen2.5-VL. Both SFT (Supervised Fine-Tuning) and reinforcement learning GRPO are used for the optimization of reasoning and language generation. We embed the updated language descriptions and feed them into a unified tracking backbone network together with vision features. Then, we adopt a tracking head to predict the specific location of the target object. In addition, we propose a large-scale long-term vision-language tracking benchmark dataset, termed TNLLT, which contains 200 video sequences. 20 baseline visual trackers are re-trained and evaluated on this dataset, which builds a solid foundation for the vision-language visual tracking task. Extensive experiments on multiple vision-language tracking benchmark datasets fully validated the effectiveness of our proposed reasoning-based natural language generation strategy. The source code of this paper will be released on https://github.com/Event-AHU/Open_VLTrack
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "ReasoningTrack: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Long-term Vision-Language Tracking."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel vision-language tracking framework called ReasoningTrack, designed to improve long-term tracking performance by incorporating chain-of-thought reasoning using a pre-trained vision-language model (Qwen2.5-VL). The method dynamically updates textual descriptions of the target object during tracking to adapt to changes in appearance or scene context. It utilizes both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with GRPO to optimize the reasoning and language generation capabilities of the model. Furthermore, the paper introduces a new large-scale long-term vision-language tracking dataset, TNLLT, containing 200 video sequences, and benchmarks several existing trackers on it. The results on TNLLT and other existing datasets show that the proposed ReasoningTrack outperforms other state-of-the-art trackers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the explicit integration of chain-of-thought reasoning into a vision-language tracking framework.  Existing methods often rely on static language descriptions or simple attention mechanisms to update them, while this paper attempts a more structured reasoning process. The approach of using both SFT and reinforcement learning to enhance the reasoning and language generation of the LLM specifically for VLT is also a key contribution. The introduction of the TNLLT dataset addresses a significant gap in the community by providing a large-scale, long-term benchmark for vision-language tracking.
    *   **Weaknesses:** While the concept is novel, the implementation appears somewhat incremental. The overall framework still relies on a traditional tracking backbone (DUTrack), and the LLM primarily acts as a language updater. The depth of the "reasoning" within the chain-of-thought is unclear from the paper and may be relatively shallow, possibly limited to high-level scene descriptions.  It's uncertain whether the "reasoning" addresses more complex issues like occlusion or significant appearance changes beyond simple descriptive updates. The paper lacks a comparison against other LLM-based update methods. While DUTrack has a method of updating language, it is not an explicit reasoner, so a direct comparison is difficult, but a comparison is warranted.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The paper addresses a critical limitation in existing vision-language tracking methods: the inability to adapt to long-term changes in the target object or scene. The TNLLT dataset has the potential to become a valuable resource for the research community, enabling more rigorous evaluation of long-term tracking algorithms. The experimental results demonstrate that ReasoningTrack can achieve state-of-the-art performance on several benchmark datasets.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The computational cost associated with using large pre-trained models is a significant drawback. The tracking speed of 15.57 fps may limit the applicability of the method to real-time applications. The fixed-interval text update strategy is a potential limitation, as it may not be suitable for all video sequences or scenarios. The method heavily depends on the quality of the initial language prompt and the capabilities of the underlying LLM. The reliance on supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning also introduces potential biases from the training data and reward function. Finally, the ablation studies, while present, could be more comprehensive. For example, the analysis of the reward function is limited.

*   **Clarity and Reproducibility:** The paper is reasonably well-written and organized. However, providing more details about the specific prompts used for the LLM, the training procedures for SFT and reinforcement learning, and the implementation details of the reward function would improve reproducibility.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to vision-language tracking by incorporating chain-of-thought reasoning. The TNLLT dataset is also a valuable contribution to the community. However, the implementation is somewhat incremental, and the computational cost is a significant drawback. Furthermore, the depth of the reasoning performed by the LLM is not entirely clear. The limitations in the method require further investigation and comparison against alternatives.

Score: 7

The paper makes a solid contribution with its introduction of chain-of-thought reasoning to VLT and its large-scale TNLLT dataset. While not entirely groundbreaking, it addresses a key weakness in existing methods and offers a promising direction for future research. It's well-executed, even with its shortcomings, but its reliance on large models and the incremental nature of the core tracking strategy lead to the score provided.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cross-LoRA: A Data-Free LoRA Transfer Framework across Heterogeneous LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05232v1)
- **Authors**: Feifan Xia, Mingyang Liao, Yuyang Fang, Defang Li, Yantong Xie, Weikang Li, Yang Li, Deguo Xia, Jizhou Huang
- **Abstract**: Traditional parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA are tightly coupled with the base model architecture, which constrains their applicability across heterogeneous pretrained large language models (LLMs). To address this limitation, we introduce Cross-LoRA, a data-free framework for transferring LoRA modules between diverse base models without requiring additional training data. Cross-LoRA consists of two key components: (a) LoRA-Align, which performs subspace alignment between source and target base models through rank-truncated singular value decomposition (SVD) and Frobenius-optimal linear transformation, ensuring compatibility under dimension mismatch; and (b) LoRA-Shift, which applies the aligned subspaces to project source LoRA weight updates into the target model parameter space. Both components are data-free, training-free, and enable lightweight adaptation on a commodity GPU in 20 minutes. Experiments on ARCs, OBOA and HellaSwag show that Cross-LoRA achieves relative gains of up to 5.26% over base models. Across other commonsense reasoning benchmarks, Cross-LoRA maintains performance comparable to that of directly trained LoRA adapters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Cross-LoRA," a novel data-free and training-free framework for transferring LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) modules between heterogeneous large language models (LLMs). Cross-LoRA addresses the limitation of standard LoRA, which is tightly coupled to the base model architecture and cannot be directly applied to other models without retraining. The framework consists of two main components: (a) "LoRA-Align," which performs subspace alignment between source and target models using rank-truncated SVD and Frobenius-optimal linear transformation to ensure compatibility across dimension mismatches, and (b) "LoRA-Shift," which projects the aligned source LoRA weight updates into the target model parameter space. The experiments demonstrate that Cross-LoRA achieves comparable or even better performance than directly trained LoRA adapters on several NLP benchmarks, while requiring minimal computational resources and no access to training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of transferring LoRA modules between heterogeneous LLMs without training data is genuinely novel. While knowledge distillation and other transfer learning techniques exist, they typically involve *some* form of training or data synthesis. Cross-LoRA's data-free and training-free approach sets it apart. The specific combination of rank-truncated SVD, Frobenius-optimal alignment, and the LoRA-Shift projection is a unique contribution.  A related work, LORA-X, also focuses on data-free transfer but requires specialized adapters. This paper removes the need for such adaptations, making it more general. ProLoRA is closer but focused on image diffusion models.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of Cross-LoRA is substantial. It offers a practical solution to the problem of adapter reuse in a world where LLMs are rapidly evolving and new base models are frequently released. The ability to transfer knowledge without retraining significantly reduces the computational cost and data requirements for adapting LLMs to new tasks and models. This is especially valuable for researchers and practitioners with limited resources. Furthermore, it opens up possibilities for a modular approach to LLM development, where adapters can be trained on specialized "learner" models and then deployed on "receiver" models without further fine-tuning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data-Free and Training-Free:** The most significant strength is the elimination of the need for training data and additional fine-tuning. This is a major advantage over traditional transfer learning methods.
    *   **Heterogeneity:** The framework's ability to handle heterogeneous LLMs (different architectures, model families) broadens its applicability.
    *   **Efficiency:** The use of rank-truncated SVD and efficient batched routines allows for lightweight transfer on commodity GPUs within a short time frame.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments demonstrate that Cross-LoRA achieves competitive performance compared to directly trained LoRA adapters on several NLP benchmarks, demonstrating its effectiveness.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the method's complexity, efficiency, and the impact of key components (e.g., subspace alignment).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Performance Gap on Some Tasks:** While Cross-LoRA performs well overall, there is a performance gap compared to directly trained LoRA on some tasks (e.g., HellaSwag), indicating that some task-specific information may be lost during projection.  The reliance on subspace alignment may not fully capture all the nuances of a task.
    *   **Architectural Sensitivity:** The effectiveness of transfer is sensitive to architectural mismatches between the source and target models.  The authors acknowledge this and provide some insights into which architectural features are more amenable to transfer. However, further research is needed to address this limitation.
    *   **One-Shot Projection:** The current framework performs a one-shot projection without any post-transfer adaptation. This may limit performance when the source and target distributions diverge significantly. A simple post-transfer fine-tuning might close the performance gap more completely, if feasible.
    *   **Limited Scalability Data:** Experimentation, while thorough, doesn't explore much beyond smaller models. It would be beneficial to show that Cross-LoRA is effective on very large models (e.g., 13B, 70B) to further solidify its practical value.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the paper provides an overview of the method's performance, it would benefit from a more detailed analysis of specific examples where Cross-LoRA fails to transfer effectively and the reasons behind these failures.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    Cross-LoRA presents a genuinely novel and practical approach to adapter transfer, addressing a significant challenge in the LLM landscape. The data-free and training-free nature of the framework, its ability to handle heterogeneity, and its efficiency are all compelling strengths. While there are limitations regarding performance gaps on some tasks, architectural sensitivity, and scalability, the authors acknowledge these limitations and suggest potential directions for future research. The potential impact of Cross-LoRA on the field is considerable, offering a practical solution for adapter reuse and a modular approach to LLM development.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification via Chain-of-Thought Enhancement and Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05234v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan Shangguan, Xiaocui Yang, Shi Feng, Daling Wang, Yifei Zhang, Ge Yu
- **Abstract**: The surge in rich multimodal content on social media platforms has greatly advanced Multimodal Sentiment Analysis (MSA), with Large Language Models (LLMs) further accelerating progress in this field. Current approaches primarily leverage the knowledge and reasoning capabilities of parameter-heavy (Multimodal) LLMs for sentiment classification, overlooking autonomous multimodal sentiment reasoning generation in resource-constrained environments. Therefore, we focus on the Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification task, JMSRC, which simultaneously performs multimodal sentiment reasoning chain generation and sentiment classification only with a lightweight model. We propose a Multimodal Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Distillation model, MulCoT-RD, designed for JMSRC that employs a "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation paradigm to address deployment constraints in resource-limited environments. We first leverage a high-performance Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) to generate the initial reasoning dataset and train a medium-sized assistant model with a multi-task learning mechanism. A lightweight student model is jointly trained to perform efficient multimodal sentiment reasoning generation and classification. Extensive experiments on four datasets demonstrate that MulCoT-RD with only 3B parameters achieves strong performance on JMSRC, while exhibiting robust generalization and enhanced interpretability.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided.

**Summary**

The paper addresses the task of Resource-Limited Joint Multimodal Sentiment Reasoning and Classification (JMSRC).  It proposes a Multimodal Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Distillation model (MulCoT-RD) designed to perform sentiment reasoning chain generation and sentiment classification using a lightweight model. The model employs a "Teacher-Assistant-Student" distillation paradigm where a high-performance MLLM (the Teacher) generates the initial reasoning dataset. This dataset is then used to train a medium-sized assistant model, followed by the training of a lightweight student model. Experiments on four datasets demonstrate that MulCoT-RD achieves strong performance on JMSRC with only 3B parameters, while exhibiting robust generalization and enhanced interpretability. The key idea is to distill reasoning capabilities from larger models into smaller, more resource-efficient models.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies in its focus on resource-constrained sentiment reasoning. While existing works utilize Large Language Models(LLMs) or other large models for MSA, this paper directly addresses the challenge of efficient deployment by distilling the reasoning capabilities of large models into smaller, more manageable ones. The specific architecture of MulCoT-RD, using the "Teacher-Assistant-Student" framework for reasoning distillation in a *joint* reasoning and classification setup, seems novel. Also, the "Multimodal CoT Enhancement Module" with its two-stage prompt design likely contributes to the novelty by enabling better reasoning generation. The adaptive replay controller is a notable detail as well.
* **Significance:** The significance of this work is substantial. Reducing the computational cost of MSA models is a critical step towards broader deployment, particularly in edge computing scenarios, or in scenarios where computational resources are limited.  The interpretability aspect is also important. By explicitly generating reasoning chains, the model provides insights into its decision-making process. The improved generalization reported in the paper points to more robust models that will likely perform better across diverse datasets. If it is as performant and robust as is claimed, it could be used across a variety of sentiment-based applications, allowing more models to be deployed to edge devices.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper tackles a relevant and important problem: resource-constrained sentiment analysis.
    * The proposed MulCoT-RD architecture is well-motivated and makes sense given the task.
    * The experimental results indicate that the model achieves strong performance and has desirable properties (generalization, interpretability).
    * The "Teacher-Assistant-Student" framework is a clever way of dealing with the limitations of both closed-source and large open-source models.
    * The paper is well written and clearly explains the proposed approach.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The model selection, even though explained, could still be viewed as limited and needs further validation. The paper does claim that the limited selection shows MulCoT-RD's usefulness.
    * The reliance on specific models (GPT-4o and Qwen) could make the approach less flexible in the long run.
    * The appendix contains a lot of needed implementation details, some of which might better be in the main body of the paper.
    * While the experiments show a great result, the lack of comparison with Emotion-LLaMA on MASC datasets is a noticeable omission. The model's architecture in this paper includes a large component of reasoning chain generation, and Emotion-LLaMA includes emotion recognition and explanation capabilities.
    * While the data augmentation with the teacher model looks good, it's still data generated by an AI. More care may need to be put into the augmented data so that it doesn't make the dataset worse.

* **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and interpretable MSA models. The "Teacher-Assistant-Student" reasoning distillation paradigm could be adopted in other tasks where distilling complex models into smaller ones is important.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of multimodal sentiment analysis by addressing the key challenge of resource efficiency. The proposed MulCoT-RD model is novel, well-designed, and shows promising experimental results. It has a clear potential to impact future research in this area, as other researchers may adapt their techniques to fit the model architecture for their purposes. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses. With more in-depth experimental comparisons, especially with comparable reasoning and understanding models such as Emotion-LLaMA, I would be more likely to rate this a 9. However, there is still a need to further validate the robustness of the distillation approach and the impact of data augmentation on the student models.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ArbiViewGen: Controllable Arbitrary Viewpoint Camera Data Generation for Autonomous Driving via Stable Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05236v1)
- **Authors**: Yatong Lan, Jingfeng Chen, Yiru Wang, Lei He
- **Abstract**: Arbitrary viewpoint image generation holds significant potential for autonomous driving, yet remains a challenging task due to the lack of ground-truth data for extrapolated views, which hampers the training of high-fidelity generative models. In this work, we propose Arbiviewgen, a novel diffusion-based framework for the generation of controllable camera images from arbitrary points of view. To address the absence of ground-truth data in unseen views, we introduce two key components: Feature-Aware Adaptive View Stitching (FAVS) and Cross-View Consistency Self-Supervised Learning (CVC-SSL). FAVS employs a hierarchical matching strategy that first establishes coarse geometric correspondences using camera poses, then performs fine-grained alignment through improved feature matching algorithms, and identifies high-confidence matching regions via clustering analysis. Building upon this, CVC-SSL adopts a self-supervised training paradigm where the model reconstructs the original camera views from the synthesized stitched images using a diffusion model, enforcing cross-view consistency without requiring supervision from extrapolated data. Our framework requires only multi-camera images and their associated poses for training, eliminating the need for additional sensors or depth maps. To our knowledge, Arbiviewgen is the first method capable of controllable arbitrary view camera image generation in multiple vehicle configurations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ArbiViewGen: Controllable Arbitrary Viewpoint Camera Data Generation for Autonomous Driving via Stable Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ArbiViewGen, a novel framework for generating controllable camera images from arbitrary viewpoints for autonomous driving. It addresses the lack of ground-truth data for extrapolated views, which hinders the training of high-fidelity generative models.  ArbiViewGen utilizes a diffusion-based approach and incorporates two key components: Feature-Aware Adaptive View Stitching (FAVS) and Cross-View Consistency Self-Supervised Learning (CVC-SSL). FAVS leverages geometric transformation and feature matching to generate pseudo-ground truth data for novel viewpoints. CVC-SSL employs a self-supervised training paradigm, reconstructing original camera views from stitched images to enforce cross-view consistency. The framework only requires multi-camera images and associated poses for training, eliminating the need for additional sensors or depth maps. The authors propose a quantitative evaluation strategy by projecting colored LiDAR point clouds to novel views.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Key Problem:** The paper tackles a critical issue in autonomous driving: the scarcity of ground-truth data for novel viewpoints, which limits the training of robust perception models.
    *   **Self-Supervised Approach:**  The CVC-SSL framework cleverly avoids the need for ground-truth data in extrapolated views by using a self-supervised learning paradigm based on cyclic reconstruction. This is a significant advantage, especially for real-world applications where obtaining such data is difficult or impossible.
    *   **Purely Visual:** The method primarily relies on visual input, enhancing applicability when additional sensors such as LiDAR or depth data are unavailable or unreliable.
    *   **Comprehensive Design:** The integration of FAVS and CVC-SSL provides a well-rounded approach. FAVS establishes a good initial alignment, while CVC-SSL refines the generated images to ensure cross-view consistency.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The proposed evaluation strategy using LiDAR point cloud projection offers a reasonable, albeit imperfect, method for quantitatively assessing the quality of the generated views.
    *   **Multi-Vehicle Architecture:** The claim that ArbiViewGen is the first method capable of controllable arbitrary view camera image generation in multiple vehicle configurations is a notable contribution to the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Multi-Camera Data:** While eliminating the need for LiDAR during inference, the method *requires* calibrated multi-camera setups for training, a requirement that can still be limiting.  The transferability to scenarios with only a single or very few cameras isn't addressed.
    *   **LiDAR-based Evaluation Limitations:** While providing a quantitative metric, the reliance on sparse LiDAR projections as "ground truth" has inherent limitations. This sparse data doesn't fully capture the richness of real-world scenes, potentially skewing the evaluation.
    *   **Geometric Simplifications:** The far-field approximation, while reasonable, might introduce errors in scenarios with nearby objects. The impact of this approximation isn't thoroughly analyzed.
    *   **Complexity:**  The framework is relatively complex, with multiple modules (FAVS, CVC-SSL, diffusion model) and careful tuning required. This complexity may hinder adoption. The ablation studies provide *some* insight into module importance, but a deeper dive into each module's sensitivity to various hyperparameters would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Core Components:** While the *integration* of these components is novel, the individual components (Stable Diffusion, feature matching, RANSAC) are relatively standard.
    *   **Qualitative Evaluation Concerns:** Though the qualitative results are presented, it can be difficult to fully discern the extent to which the generated imagery corresponds with true driving scenes given the limitations on the validation procedure due to the lack of real novel viewpoint images.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Potential for Data Augmentation:** ArbiViewGen holds significant potential for data augmentation in autonomous driving, allowing for the generation of diverse training data from limited real-world recordings.
    *   **Cross-Platform Data Reuse:** The ability to generate views from arbitrary viewpoints facilitates cross-platform data reuse, reducing the need for extensive data collection for each new vehicle model.
    *   **Overcoming Data Scarcity:** The self-supervised learning approach can help overcome the challenge of data scarcity in challenging driving scenarios.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a critical problem and proposing a novel, self-supervised framework. The strengths of the paper lie in its approach to tackle the data scarcity problem and to the ability of generating new multi-view images for training. This framework is potentially useful in areas of cross-platform generalization and data augmentation. However, the paper suffers from reliance on a multi-camera system, complex design, and the limitations of LiDAR-based evaluation, and the relatively minimal innovations with respect to baseline models within each respective part of the model design. Therefore, the score is an 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Driver Assistant: Persuading Drivers to Adjust Secondary Tasks Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05238v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Xiang, Muchen Li, Jie Yan, Manling Zheng, Hanfei Zhu, Mengyun Jiang, Lingyun Sun
- **Abstract**: Level 3 automated driving systems allows drivers to engage in secondary tasks while diminishing their perception of risk. In the event of an emergency necessitating driver intervention, the system will alert the driver with a limited window for reaction and imposing a substantial cognitive burden. To address this challenge, this study employs a Large Language Model (LLM) to assist drivers in maintaining an appropriate attention on road conditions through a "humanized" persuasive advice. Our tool leverages the road conditions encountered by Level 3 systems as triggers, proactively steering driver behavior via both visual and auditory routes. Empirical study indicates that our tool is effective in sustaining driver attention with reduced cognitive load and coordinating secondary tasks with takeover behavior. Our work provides insights into the potential of using LLMs to support drivers during multi-task automated driving.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of a Large Language Model (LLM) as a "driving assistant" to proactively persuade drivers in Level 3 automated driving systems to maintain attention to road conditions and reduce distraction from secondary tasks. It tackles the problem of drivers becoming disengaged and cognitively burdened when needing to take over control. The approach involves using road conditions as triggers for persuasive advice delivered through both visual and auditory channels.  The study includes a formative user study to understand driver preferences for persuasion strategies, development of an LLM-based tool, and an empirical evaluation with 40 participants validating the tool's effectiveness in maintaining driver attention, reducing cognitive load, and coordinating secondary tasks with takeover behavior. The authors compared their system against conventional takeover request audio alerts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a potentially novel approach by integrating LLMs for real-time persuasive interventions in Level 3 automated driving scenarios. While persuasion techniques have been explored in the context of driving before, the use of LLMs to dynamically tailor advice based on a combination of road conditions, driver state (distraction level), and user preferences adds a layer of sophistication. The comparison to standard alert systems provides a good baseline. The incorporation of a 'character' displaying facial cues, to communicate risk, may also present a novel design approach.

*   **Significance:**  The research addresses a critical safety concern in the transition to higher levels of automated driving – maintaining driver engagement and preparedness. A system that can subtly and proactively guide driver behavior, rather than relying solely on abrupt warnings, holds the promise of reducing cognitive load and improving reaction times during critical events. The paper's findings, suggesting reduced engagement in secondary tasks and improved attention allocation, indicate a practical benefit. The formative user study to customize the approach is an important element of user-centered design and enhances the potential impact of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem it addresses and its importance.
    *   **User-Centered Approach:** The formative study to understand driver preferences is a strength, ensuring the system is designed with user needs in mind.
    *   **LLM Integration:** The paper effectively leverages LLM capabilities for adaptive and context-aware persuasion.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The empirical study provides some initial evidence supporting the tool's effectiveness, with both objective (eye-tracking data, secondary task frequency) and subjective (questionnaire responses) measures. The statistical significance for several key measures strengthens the claims.
    *   **Comparison with Baseline:** The comparison against takeover alerts provides a basis for assessment of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Virtual Environment:**  The experiment takes place in a simulated environment. While necessary for initial testing, the transferability of these results to real-world driving scenarios needs further investigation. This is a significant limitation because it lacks the complexities, unpredictability, and emotional states generated in real-world driving. The generalizability of the findings may be limited to similar simulated scenarios.
    *   **Sample Size:** A sample size of 36 participants is reasonable, but a larger sample would increase the statistical power and generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Risk Factors:** Further elaboration of the specific risk factors used by LLM in the scenarios might increase credibility. The road conditions can be more granular.
    *   **Long-Term Effects:** The study only examines immediate effects of the persuasion system. The long-term impact of such a system on driver behavior and acceptance remains unclear.
    *   **The novelty of some elements is questionable:** Persuasion for safety has been investigated for a long time, albeit without recent models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper contributes to the growing body of research on human-machine interaction in automated driving. It offers a potential direction for designing more proactive and personalized driver assistance systems. The insights into how to use LLMs for persuasive interventions can be valuable for other researchers in the field. However, the current impact and future influence depend on addressing the limitations of the simulation.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a promising exploration of using LLMs to proactively manage driver attention in Level 3 automated vehicles. While the virtual environment limits the generalizability of the results, the user-centered design, LLM integration, and empirical validation establish a solid foundation for future research.

Score: 6.5

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the novelty and significance of the work, tempered by the limitations of the experimental setup. The idea of leveraging LLMs for dynamic persuasive interventions in driving is innovative. However, the reliance on a virtual environment and the need for further validation in real-world scenarios reduce the overall impact. The solid research methodology justifies a score above average, but the caveats restrict the rating from being higher. A score of 6.5 acknowledges the contribution while highlighting the need for future research to establish the system's effectiveness in more ecologically valid settings.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Pruning Large Language Models by Identifying and Preserving Functional Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05239v1)
- **Authors**: Yiheng Liu, Junhao Ning, Sichen Xia, Xiaohui Gao, Ning Qiang, Bao Ge, Junwei Han, Xintao Hu
- **Abstract**: Structured pruning is one of the representative techniques for compressing large language models (LLMs) to reduce GPU memory consumption and accelerate inference speed. It offers significant practical value in improving the efficiency of LLMs in real-world applications. Current structured pruning methods typically rely on assessment of the importance of the structure units and pruning the units with less importance. Most of them overlooks the interaction and collaboration among artificial neurons that are crucial for the functionalities of LLMs, leading to a disruption in the macro functional architecture of LLMs and consequently a pruning performance degradation. Inspired by the inherent similarities between artificial neural networks and functional neural networks in the human brain, we alleviate this challenge and propose to prune LLMs by identifying and preserving functional networks within LLMs in this study. To achieve this, we treat an LLM as a digital brain and decompose the LLM into functional networks, analogous to identifying functional brain networks in neuroimaging data. Afterwards, an LLM is pruned by preserving the key neurons within these functional networks. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method can successfully identify and locate functional networks and key neurons in LLMs, enabling efficient model pruning. Our code is available at https://github.com/WhatAboutMyStar/LLM_ACTIVATION.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a structured pruning method for large language models (LLMs) that draws inspiration from cognitive neuroscience.  The central idea is to identify and preserve functional networks within LLMs, analogous to identifying functional brain networks in human brain neuroimaging data (fMRI). The authors treat an LLM as a "digital brain" and decompose it into functional networks using Independent Component Analysis (ICA), mimicking fMRI analysis. By pruning the model while preserving these identified functional networks, the method aims to minimize disruption to the LLM's architecture and maintain performance after pruning. The method is evaluated on multiple benchmark datasets and compared against existing structured pruning techniques. The results suggest that the proposed approach achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance in several tasks while reducing model size and computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in adapting techniques from cognitive neuroscience, specifically fMRI analysis and ICA, to the problem of pruning LLMs. This is a relatively unique perspective that differs from traditional importance-based or data-driven pruning approaches. While the individual components (ICA, pruning) are not novel in themselves, their combination and application within the LLM context represent a novel contribution. Applying ICA, an established fMRI analysis technique, to identify coherent functional networks within LLMs is insightful. It moves beyond merely evaluating individual neuron importance to consider group behavior and interaction, potentially aligning more closely with how LLMs actually function.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the LLM field: reducing the computational cost of large models. By proposing a method that can effectively prune LLMs without severely impacting performance, the research makes a valuable contribution towards making LLMs more accessible and deployable in resource-constrained environments.  If the findings are robust and generalizable, they could significantly impact how LLMs are compressed and optimized. The focus on preserving "functional networks" offers a more nuanced approach compared to brute-force pruning, potentially leading to better long-term performance and stability.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Brain-inspired approach:** The analogy between LLMs and the human brain is a compelling and potentially fruitful direction for research.
    * **Clear Methodology:** The paper outlines the steps of the proposed method clearly, making it relatively easy to understand and replicate.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper includes empirical results on multiple datasets, showing that the proposed method performs competitively with or outperforms other existing pruning techniques.
    * **Ablation studies:** The paper provides some ablation study by introducing/removing bias terms and also includes experiment on the number of components to use in CanICA.
    * **Multiple Model Evaluation:** The results are validated across multiple models, Vicuna-7B-v1.5, LLAMA2-7B-chat-hf and ChatGLM3-6B-base.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simplified Neuroscience Analogy:** While the brain analogy is compelling, it's inherently a simplification. The complex interactions and non-linearities in biological neural networks are likely far more intricate than what's captured by ICA in an LLM. The paper needs to clearly acknowledge the limitations of this analogy.
    * **ICA Limitations:** ICA is a linear method, as pointed out by the authors themselves. This might not fully capture the non-linear interactions present in LLMs, limiting the effectiveness of functional network identification.
    * **Computational Cost of ICA:** Performing ICA on very large models can be computationally expensive. The paper needs to elaborate on the computational cost implications and potential optimizations. While layer-by-layer pruning is proposed for reducing the dimension, there is little detail provided on the implementation specifics.
    * **Lack of Interpretability:** While identifying "functional networks" is a positive step, the paper does not provide much insight into what these networks actually *do*. Understanding the function and purpose of these preserved networks would further strengthen the significance of the research.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** It is known that ICA is sensitive to hyperparameters like the number of components. Choosing the number of networks from fMRI analysis may not necessarily translate effectively to LLMs. How these hyperparameters interact with the model size, datasets and training methods is not covered.

* **Potential Impact:** If the method proves robust and generalizable across different LLM architectures and tasks, it could become a valuable tool for model compression.  The brain-inspired approach could also inspire other research directions in LLM design and optimization.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and interesting approach to structured pruning of LLMs, drawing inspiration from cognitive neuroscience. The methodology is clearly described, and the empirical results are promising, showing competitive performance against existing methods. However, the reliance on a simplified brain analogy, potential limitations of ICA, and the lack of interpretability of the identified functional networks limit the overall significance. The paper contributes a novel methodology and promising results, but further investigation and validation are required to fully assess its impact on the field. A higher score would be warranted with more in-depth analysis of the discovered networks, a more critical discussion of the brain analogy limitations, and experiments on larger models to demonstrate scalability.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CodeBoost: Boosting Code LLMs by Squeezing Knowledge from Code Snippets with RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05242v1)
- **Authors**: Sijie Wang, Quanjiang Guo, Kai Zhao, Yawei Zhang, Xin Li, Xiang Li, Siqi Li, Rui She, Shangshu Yu, Wee Peng Tay
- **Abstract**: Code large language models (LLMs) have become indispensable tools for building efficient and automated coding pipelines. Existing models are typically post-trained using reinforcement learning (RL) from general-purpose LLMs using "human instruction-final answer" pairs, where the instructions are usually from manual annotations. However, collecting high-quality coding instructions is both labor-intensive and difficult to scale. On the other hand, code snippets are abundantly available from various sources. This imbalance presents a major bottleneck in instruction-based post-training. We propose CodeBoost, a post-training framework that enhances code LLMs purely from code snippets, without relying on human-annotated instructions. CodeBoost introduces the following key components: (1) maximum-clique curation, which selects a representative and diverse training corpus from code; (2) bi-directional prediction, which enables the model to learn from both forward and backward prediction objectives; (3) error-aware prediction, which incorporates learning signals from both correct and incorrect outputs; (4) heterogeneous augmentation, which diversifies the training distribution to enrich code semantics; and (5) heterogeneous rewarding, which guides model learning through multiple reward types including format correctness and execution feedback from both successes and failures. Extensive experiments across several code LLMs and benchmarks verify that CodeBoost consistently improves performance, demonstrating its effectiveness as a scalable and effective training pipeline.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CodeBoost: Boosting Code LLMs by Squeezing Knowledge from Code Snippets with RL" introduces a post-training framework designed to enhance code LLMs using only raw code snippets, eliminating the need for human-annotated instructions.  CodeBoost incorporates several key components: (1) maximum-clique curation to select a diverse and representative training corpus; (2) bi-directional prediction, enabling the model to learn from both forward and backward prediction objectives; (3) error-aware prediction, incorporating learning signals from both correct and incorrect outputs; (4) heterogeneous augmentation to diversify the training distribution; and (5) heterogeneous rewarding to guide model learning.  The authors conduct experiments across various code LLMs and benchmarks, demonstrating consistent performance improvements.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the approach of post-training code LLMs *without* relying on human-annotated instructions. While techniques like SFT and RL have been employed for post-training before, they typically hinge on instruction-answer pairs. CodeBoost tackles the scalability issue of obtaining such data by leveraging readily available code snippets and designing training tasks to generate pseudo-instructions directly. The maximum-clique curation for dataset diversification and the error-aware prediction are also valuable contributions. The integration of all these elements into a single pipeline, specifically tailored for code LLMs, provides a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve code LLMs' performance while bypassing the bottleneck of human annotation.  If effective at scale, CodeBoost could significantly reduce the cost and effort involved in training and improving code generation models. The empirical results, demonstrating performance improvements across multiple code LLMs and benchmarks, support this potential. The framework itself, along with the individual components (bi-directional prediction, error-aware learning, etc.) offer valuable insights and potentially reusable techniques for the code LLM community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a crucial problem:**  The shortage of high-quality instruction data is a significant challenge in code LLM training.
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** The paper integrates several innovative techniques into a cohesive pipeline.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The performance improvements are demonstrated across various LLMs and benchmarks, showcasing the robustness and generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Well-defined components:**  Each component of CodeBoost is clearly explained and justified.
    *   **Scalability potential:**  The instruction-free nature of the training makes the pipeline scalable to massive code datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on execution:** CodeBoost relies heavily on the ability to execute code snippets in a sandbox environment.  This might be problematic for certain code types (e.g., those with external dependencies or that require specific hardware).
    *   **Limited ablation study depth:** While the ablation study shows the contribution of each module, it could be more in-depth, for example, exploring the impact of different reward function configurations or varying the parameters of the maximum-clique selection.
    *   **Focus on Python:** The experiments are primarily focused on Python code, limiting the immediate generalizability to other programming languages. While the concepts are potentially transferable, adaptations might be necessary.
    *   **Potential for bias:** While attempting to mitigate the issue through techniques like maximum clique curation, the curated training set is still constructed from available open-source code and might suffer from subtle biases present in the original data. This could potentially impact LLMs in specific contexts, resulting in outputs that align with existing biases within training materials.

*   **Potential Influence:** CodeBoost has the potential to influence the training methodologies of code LLMs, shifting the focus from instruction-based approaches to snippet-based learning. The individual techniques presented (e.g., the error-aware prediction, bi-directional prediction and maximum-clique curation) could be adopted and adapted by other researchers.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and practical solution to a pressing problem in code LLM training. The results demonstrate a clear benefit to adopting CodeBoost, and the individual components offer valuable contributions. The approach is not without limitations, particularly the reliance on code execution and focus on Python. Therefore, while innovative and impactful, it falls short of being truly groundbreaking. The score reflects the novelty and effectiveness, but recognizes the weaknesses and the need for further research to address the identified limitations and fully unlock the generalizability and robustness of the pipeline.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Salt-Rock Creep Deformation Forecasting Using Deep Neural Networks and Analytical Models for Subsurface Energy Storage Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05248v1)
- **Authors**: Pradeep Kumar Shukla, Tanujit Chakraborty, Mustafa Sari, Joel Sarout, Partha Pratim Mandal
- **Abstract**: This study provides an in-depth analysis of time series forecasting methods to predict the time-dependent deformation trend (also known as creep) of salt rock under varying confining pressure conditions. Creep deformation assessment is essential for designing and operating underground storage facilities for nuclear waste, hydrogen energy, or radioactive materials. Salt rocks, known for their mechanical properties like low porosity, low permeability, high ductility, and exceptional creep and self-healing capacities, were examined using multi-stage triaxial (MSTL) creep data. After resampling, axial strain datasets were recorded at 5--10 second intervals under confining pressure levels ranging from 5 to 35 MPa over 5.8--21 days. Initial analyses, including Seasonal-Trend Decomposition (STL) and Granger causality tests, revealed minimal seasonality and causality between axial strain and temperature data. Further statistical tests, such as the Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF) test, confirmed the stationarity of the data with p-values less than 0.05, and wavelet coherence plot (WCP) analysis indicated repeating trends. A suite of deep neural network (DNN) models (Neural Basis Expansion Analysis for Time Series (N-BEATS), Temporal Convolutional Networks (TCN), Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN), and Transformers (TF)) was utilized and compared against statistical baseline models. Predictive performance was evaluated using Root Mean Square Error (RMSE), Mean Absolute Error (MAE), Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), and Symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (SMAPE). Results demonstrated that N-BEATS and TCN models outperformed others across various stress levels, respectively. DNN models, particularly N-BEATS and TCN, showed a 15--20\% improvement in accuracy over traditional analytical models, effectively capturing complex temporal dependencies and patterns.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of deep neural networks (DNNs) and analytical models for forecasting the time-dependent deformation (creep) of salt rock under varying confining pressures. The study uses multi-stage triaxial creep data obtained from salt rock samples, analyzes the data for seasonality and stationarity, and compares the performance of various DNN models (N-BEATS, TCN, RNN, Transformers) against statistical (ES, TBATS, ARIMA, Theta) and analytical models (power law, Kelvin, Burger, etc.).  The results indicate that N-BEATS and TCN models outperform other models in predicting creep behavior, demonstrating a 15-20% improvement in accuracy compared to traditional analytical models. The paper also includes a conformal prediction analysis with 95% prediction intervals.  Finally, the paper analyzes model accuracy utilizing multiple statistical tools, such as RMSE, MAE, MAPE, and SMAPE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Model Comparison:** The paper presents a broad comparison of various time-series forecasting methods, including advanced DNNs, traditional statistical models, and analytical approaches.  This allows for a thorough assessment of the strengths and weaknesses of each method for predicting salt rock creep.
    *   **Focus on a Practical Problem:**  Forecasting creep in salt rock is a critical aspect of designing and operating underground storage facilities. The research directly addresses a real-world engineering challenge.
    *   **Use of Multi-Stage Triaxial Data:**  The use of experimental creep data from multi-stage triaxial tests strengthens the study's practical relevance.
    *   **Rigorous Validation:** The models were evaluated using several metrics (RMSE, MAE, MAPE, SMAPE) and a multiple comparisons test (MCB), enhancing the reliability and robustness of the findings. The inclusion of conformal prediction adds another layer of model evaluation.
    *   **Identifies specific Model advantages:** The N-BEATS model excelled in short-term forecasting, whereas the TCN model proved superior in forecasting over longer durations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Consideration of External Factors:** The study simplifies the analysis by primarily focusing on axial strain as the predictor variable. While temperature was mentioned and initially considered, it was eventually disregarded due to minimal observed causality. However, more complex models could benefit from the inclusion of other external influences (e.g., stress state, saturation) as predictors, which may have a substantial impact on creep behaviour.
    *   **Room Temperature Tests:** The tests are conducted at room temperature. Creep behavior is significantly influenced by temperature, so the findings might not directly apply to underground storage conditions at varying temperatures.
    *   **Limited Dataset:** Some mention of the transformer having limitations in model accuracy due to a limited data set.
    *   **Limited time scales**: All of the reported results are under 22 days. While there is mention of applying analytical solutions for long term analysis, the limited experimental data undermines this analysis.
    *  **Lack of Justification for Model Hyperparameters:** While the paper lists hypertunning parameter values, there is little justification for the specific parameters chosen. This lack of explanation weakens the paper.
    *  **Presentation Problems:** Figure 5 appears to be incomplete, adding to the lack of justification for Model hyperparameter values.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    While the application of DNNs to time-series forecasting is not entirely novel, the specific application to salt rock creep and the comprehensive comparison of models contribute to the field. The clear demonstration of the superiority of N-BEATS and TCN models over traditional methods in this context is a significant finding.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The research can guide engineers and geoscientists in selecting appropriate forecasting methods for creep behavior in salt rock, leading to more reliable designs and safer operation of underground storage facilities.
    *   The study can motivate further research into the use of DNNs for other geomechanical problems.
    *   Findings can encourage the inclusion of more complex predictors into future models.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the effectiveness of DNNs for forecasting salt rock creep. The experimental data adds significance, however the limited time scales and environmental considerations undermine the study. The comprehensive model comparison and validation is strong. But, the limited datasets, lack of justifcation for Model hyperparameter values, and presentation problems detract. A score of 6 reflects a solid contribution with practical implications, but also limitations that prevent it from being a truly exceptional or transformative work.

- **Classification**: physics.geo-ph
- **Score**: 6/10

### MoBE: Mixture-of-Basis-Experts for Compressing MoE-based LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05257v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaodong Chen, Mingming Ha, Zhenzhong Lan, Jing Zhang, Jianguo Li
- **Abstract**: The Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture has become a predominant paradigm for scaling large language models (LLMs). Despite offering strong performance and computational efficiency, large MoE-based LLMs like DeepSeek-V3-0324 and Kimi-K2-Instruct present serious challenges due to substantial memory requirements in deployment. While recent works have explored MoE compression to address this issue, existing methods often suffer from considerable accuracy drops (e.g., 7-14% relatively) even at modest compression rates. This paper introduces a novel Mixture-of-Basis-Experts (MoBE) method that achieves model compression while incurring minimal accuracy drops. Specifically, each up/gate matrix in an expert is decomposed via a rank decomposition as W = AB, where matrix A is unique to each expert. The relatively larger matrix B is further re-parameterized as a linear combination of basis matrices {Bi} shared across all experts within a given MoE layer. The factorization is learned by minimizing the reconstruction error relative to the original weight matrices. Experiments demonstrate that MoBE achieves notably lower accuracy drops compared to prior works. For instance, MoBE can reduce the parameter counts of Qwen3-235B-A22B-2507, DeepSeek-V3-0324 (671B) and Kimi-K2-Instruct (1T) by 24%-30% with only 1%-2% accuracy drop (about 2% drops when measured relatively).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Mixture-of-Basis-Experts (MoBE), a novel method for compressing Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) based Large Language Models (LLMs). MoBE achieves compression by factorizing the up/gate matrix in each expert into two matrices, A and B (W = AB). A is unique to each expert while the larger matrix B is further re-parameterized as a linear combination of basis matrices shared across all experts within a MoE layer. The factorization is learned by minimizing the reconstruction error with respect to the original weights. The authors demonstrate that MoBE achieves significant parameter reduction (24%-30%) on models like Qwen3, DeepSeek-V3, and Kimi-K2 with only a small accuracy drop (1%-2%). MoBE exhibits superior performance compared to existing MoE compression techniques such as MoLAE and D2-MoE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of MoBE, factorizing the expert matrices into a unique expert-specific matrix and a linear combination of shared basis matrices, is indeed a novel approach to MoE compression. While matrix factorization and weight sharing are common techniques, their specific combination and application within the MoE framework, particularly with the focus on minimizing reconstruction error, contributes a new perspective. The idea of using shared basis matrices to capture common information across experts is compelling.
*   **Significance:** The problem addressed – compressing large MoE-based LLMs for easier deployment – is highly relevant. MoE models offer performance and computational efficiency, but their memory footprint remains a significant hurdle. MoBE offers a potential solution that balances compression and accuracy, making it a valuable contribution to the field. The empirical results, demonstrating reduced parameter counts with minimal accuracy loss compared to existing methods, reinforces the significance of the work. The thorough empirical evaluation across different MoE models adds to the credibility of the results.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Approach:** The MoBE architecture is well-motivated and introduces a useful combination of techniques.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper presents comprehensive results on various MoE models, demonstrating the effectiveness of MoBE. The consistent outperformance compared to MoLAE and D2-MoE is significant.
    *   **Clear Explanations:** The methodology is clearly explained, making it easy to understand the MoBE approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The inclusion of ablation studies, specifically on activation functions and normalization, strengthens the analysis and provides insights into the design choices.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Implementation Complexity:** The paper mentions the increase in computation caused by mimicing factorization with kernel-fused MoE and the limitations in implementing a specific mega-kernel to improve efficiency.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While MoBE is novel, it builds upon existing ideas like matrix factorization and weight sharing.  The improvement over existing methods, while consistent, might be seen as incremental rather than revolutionary. Although there is a clear performance improvement.
    *   **Focus on Downstream Performance:** The paper rightly prioritizes downstream task performance, but a more in-depth analysis of the reconstructed weight matrices (beyond MSE) could provide further insight into what MoBE preserves and discards.

**Potential Influence:**

MoBE has the potential to influence the field of LLM compression by offering a practical and effective technique for reducing the memory footprint of MoE models. The shared basis expert approach could inspire further research into alternative weight sharing and parameterization strategies. The open-sourcing of the code can promote further exploration, refinement, and integration into existing LLM frameworks.
The paper's value lies in its actionable approach, providing a clear recipe for implementing a more efficient MoE compression strategy that could be applied to a range of different models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**
MoBE represents a solid contribution to the field of LLM compression.  The idea is novel and builds well upon prior work, showing significant and consistent improvements over other methods. The paper is well-written, with complete implementation details and ablation studies to illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. The primary reason for not assigning a higher score (8+) is that while the performance improvements are significant, the technique itself, the usage of matrix factorization and linear combination of shared basis matrices are building upon existing work rather than being radically new. Nevertheless, its practical significance and strong empirical results justify a 7.5, suggesting it has the potential to make a real-world impact and also influence future work in the compression of language models.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SGDFuse: SAM-Guided Diffusion for High-Fidelity Infrared and Visible Image Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05264v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyang Zhang, Zhen Hua, Yakun Ju, Wei Zhou, Jun Liu, Alex C. Kot
- **Abstract**: Infrared and visible image fusion (IVIF) aims to combine the thermal radiation information from infrared images with the rich texture details from visible images to enhance perceptual capabilities for downstream visual tasks. However, existing methods often fail to preserve key targets due to a lack of deep semantic understanding of the scene, while the fusion process itself can also introduce artifacts and detail loss, severely compromising both image quality and task performance.
