The paper advocates for the use of sampling frequency as a viable substitute for logit-based probabilities in black-box scenarios, offering a model-agnostic framework for reliable uncertainty quantification in MCQA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a significant problem:** The unreliability of LLMs, particularly in high-stakes applications, is a pressing issue. Uncertainty quantification is crucial for building trustworthy systems.
    *   **Novel approach:**  The idea of using sampling frequency to estimate uncertainty in black-box LLMs is a simple but potentially effective approach. It tackles the challenge that traditional logit-based uncertainty measures are inaccessible in many commercial LLM APIs.
    *   **Solid methodology:** The use of conformal prediction to provide provable coverage guarantees adds rigor to the approach and makes the results more trustworthy.  The experimental setup appears to be reasonably comprehensive, using multiple models and datasets.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The results demonstrate the effectiveness of the frequency-based PE, with improvements in AUROC compared to logit-based methods in certain cases. The control of empirical miscoverage rate also supports the validity of the approach.
    *   **Model-Agnostic and Distribution-Free:** Leveraging CP allows for a model-agnostic approach, which is highly desirable for practical applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental novelty:** While the approach is clever, the overall novelty might be somewhat incremental. Conformal Prediction is already a well-established technique and adapting it to use sampling frequency, while practical, might not be a groundbreaking contribution.
    *   **Limited gains:** While the experiments show positive results, the improvements in AUROC are relatively small in some cases (e.g., 2% higher in one specific scenario). The practical significance of these small gains needs to be considered.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The method relies on multiple independent samplings, which could be computationally expensive, especially for large models and complex tasks. This practical limitation is not thoroughly discussed.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity**: The paper doesn't address the sensitivity of the performance with respect to the number of samples *M* taken. If the performance varies significantly with different values of *M*, then it would affect the robustness of the proposed method.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper offers a practical solution for uncertainty quantification in black-box LLMs, which is particularly relevant given the increasing reliance on API-based access to these models.
    *   The use of conformal prediction provides a way to build more trustworthy LLM-based systems with quantifiable guarantees.
    *   The work can stimulate further research into alternative methods for uncertainty quantification in LLMs, particularly those that do not rely on internal model parameters.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a contribution to the important problem of uncertainty quantification in LLMs. The proposed method of using sampling frequency as a proxy for logits within the CP framework provides a practical approach to model unreliability when using black-box APIs. While it's a clever adaptation of existing techniques rather than a revolutionary breakthrough, it offers a useful and empirically validated method. However, the small gains in performance and the lack of discussion of computational cost and hyperparameter sensitivity limits the impact of the paper.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### PRvL: Quantifying the Capabilities and Risks of Large Language Models for PII Redaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05545v1)
- **Authors**: Leon Garza, Anantaa Kotal, Aritran Piplai, Lavanya Elluri, Prajit Das, Aman Chadha
- **Abstract**: Redacting Personally Identifiable Information (PII) from unstructured text is critical for ensuring data privacy in regulated domains. While earlier approaches have relied on rule-based systems and domain-specific Named Entity Recognition (NER) models, these methods fail to generalize across formats and contexts. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising alternative, yet the effect of architectural and training choices on redaction performance remains underexplored. LLMs have demonstrated strong performance in tasks that require contextual language understanding, including the redaction of PII in free-form text. Prior work suggests that with appropriate adaptation, LLMs can become effective contextual privacy learners. However, the consequences of architectural and training choices for PII Redaction remain underexplored. In this work, we present a comprehensive analysis of LLMs as privacy-preserving PII Redaction systems. We evaluate a range of LLM architectures and training strategies for their effectiveness in PII Redaction. Our analysis measures redaction performance, semantic preservation, and PII leakage, and compares these outcomes against latency and computational cost. The results provide practical guidance for configuring LLM-based redactors that are accurate, efficient, and privacy-aware. To support reproducibility and real-world deployment, we release PRvL, an open-source suite of fine-tuned models, and evaluation tools for general-purpose PII Redaction. PRvL is built entirely on open-source LLMs and supports multiple inference settings for flexibility and compliance. It is designed to be easily customized for different domains and fully operable within secure, self-managed environments. This enables data owners to perform redactions without relying on third-party services or exposing sensitive content beyond their own infrastructure.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PRvL: Quantifying the Capabilities and Risks of Large Language Models for PII Redaction":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for Personally Identifiable Information (PII) redaction. It systematically evaluates various LLM architectures, training paradigms (fine-tuning, instruction tuning, RAG), and inference strategies to determine their effectiveness in accurately redacting PII from unstructured text. The evaluation focuses on redaction performance (span correctness, label exactness), semantic preservation (ROUGE, BLEU), and privacy leakage (SPriV). The authors release PRvL, an open-source toolkit consisting of fine-tuned models and evaluation tools to facilitate reproducibility and real-world deployment. The key findings indicate that instruction-tuned models, particularly smaller ones like DeepSeek-Q1, offer a good balance between accuracy, efficiency, and privacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic and comprehensive evaluation of different LLM architectures, training methods, and inference strategies *specifically* for PII redaction. While previous work has explored LLMs for privacy tasks, including de-identification and redaction, this paper stands out for its rigorous comparative analysis across a diverse set of open-source models (including more recent models like Llama 3 and DeepSeek), training approaches (fine-tuning, instruction tuning, and RAG), and several languages.  The release of PRvL as an open-source toolkit is also a significant contribution, allowing for reproducibility and wider adoption of the research.  The custom evaluation metrics (structural edit distance with semantic interpretation) are also a novel attempt to better measure redaction performance compared to standard NER metrics.

However, the *individual* components aren't entirely novel. Using LLMs for text processing tasks, including NER, is well-established. RAG and instruction tuning are also known techniques. The novelty arises from their specific application, systematic combination, and evaluation in the PII redaction context. The novelty also resides in their fine-tuned model checkpoints and benchmarking results.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a very important and timely problem: ensuring data privacy in the age of LLMs. PII redaction is crucial in regulated industries, and the paper provides valuable insights into how to effectively leverage LLMs for this task while mitigating potential privacy risks.  The open-source PRvL toolkit has the potential to significantly impact the field by providing a practical and auditable solution for PII redaction. The empirical findings provide practical guidance for practitioners in selecting the appropriate models and training strategies for different resource constraints and performance requirements. The cross-lingual evaluations also offer important insights into the generalizability of LLM-based redaction systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a rigorous and multi-faceted evaluation, considering accuracy, semantic preservation, privacy leakage, latency, and resource requirements.
*   **Open-Source Toolkit:** The release of PRvL is a significant contribution that promotes reproducibility and real-world deployment.
*   **Practical Guidance:**  The paper provides practical recommendations for selecting and configuring LLMs for PII redaction based on experimental findings.
*   **Analysis of Error Modes:**  The taxonomy of redaction errors provides valuable insights into the limitations of different models.
*   **Attention to Privacy:** SPriV is used to analyze the privacy leakage risk of each models.
*   **Cross-Lingual analysis:** By analyzing the cross-lingual performance of each model, the robustness is better shown.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset limitations:** Although the dataset is stated to be contextually rich, it is also stated to be synthetic. As such, the performance of models may not be completely reflective of how well they perform in a real world application.
*   **Limited evaluation of advanced prompting techniques:** The prompt engineering and RAG analysis could be more extensive. Exploring few-shot learning with different prompt structures or more sophisticated retrieval strategies may be of interest.
*   **SPriV is a crude metric:** SPriV only analyzes direct leakage based on overlap with the prompt. This may not be adequate, as complex leakage is not taken into consideration. More complex privacy analysis such as differential privacy or membership inference is needed.
*   **Reliance on LoRA:** The model is optimized to use LoRA, implying that models that perform poorly with LoRA may have better potential.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence the field by:

*   Providing a benchmark for evaluating LLMs for PII redaction.
*   Guiding practitioners in selecting and configuring LLMs for PII redaction.
*   Inspiring further research into privacy-preserving adaptation techniques for LLMs.
*   Encouraging the development of more sophisticated evaluation metrics for PII redaction and privacy leakage.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and systematic study of LLMs for PII redaction with a very useful contribution of an open-source toolkit. It combines existing techniques in a novel way and rigorously evaluates their performance in the target task. It is well-written and provides actionable insights for practitioners. However, the paper does rely on relatively well-known LLM training and inference techniques, although they are combined in a novel setting with carefully crafted experiments. Further, the SPriV metric should be replaced by something more comprehensive. Despite these weaknesses, the paper makes a meaningful contribution to the field and has the potential to significantly impact how PII redaction is performed in practice.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Do Political Opinions Transfer Between Western Languages? An Analysis of Unaligned and Aligned Multilingual LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05553v1)
- **Authors**: Franziska Weeber, Tanise Ceron, Sebastian Padó
- **Abstract**: Public opinion surveys show cross-cultural differences in political opinions between socio-cultural contexts. However, there is no clear evidence whether these differences translate to cross-lingual differences in multilingual large language models (MLLMs). We analyze whether opinions transfer between languages or whether there are separate opinions for each language in MLLMs of various sizes across five Western languages. We evaluate MLLMs' opinions by prompting them to report their (dis)agreement with political statements from voting advice applications. To better understand the interaction between languages in the models, we evaluate them both before and after aligning them with more left or right views using direct preference optimization and English alignment data only. Our findings reveal that unaligned models show only very few significant cross-lingual differences in the political opinions they reflect. The political alignment shifts opinions almost uniformly across all five languages. We conclude that in Western language contexts, political opinions transfer between languages, demonstrating the challenges in achieving explicit socio-linguistic, cultural, and political alignment of MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether political opinions in multilingual large language models (MLLMs) transfer between Western languages or if the models maintain separate, language-specific opinions. The authors evaluate several MLLMs of varying sizes across German, English, French, Spanish, and Italian. They prompt the models to express agreement or disagreement with political statements derived from voting advice applications. Crucially, they examine the models both before and after aligning them with more left- or right-leaning views using direct preference optimization (DPO) and English alignment data. The study reveals that unaligned models show minimal cross-lingual differences in their political opinions. More importantly, political alignment through English data shifts opinions almost uniformly across all five languages. The authors conclude that within the Western language context, political opinions tend to transfer between languages in MLLMs, underscoring challenges in achieving explicit socio-linguistic and political alignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper tackles an important issue – the cross-lingual transfer of political biases in MLLMs. Prior works have studied political biases, but the cross-lingual aspect and particularly the analysis of alignment effects *across* languages, using *English alignment*, is relatively novel. While some studies have compared biases across languages, the systematic investigation of *transfer* mechanisms through alignment manipulation adds a valuable dimension.  The use of DPO with a nuanced political dataset is a good choice, and the robustness checks are a solid addition. However, the core finding – that opinions transfer – isn't wholly unexpected, given the shared pretraining data and multilingual alignment already present in these models.

**Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in its empirical demonstration of opinion transfer and the difficulty of achieving true language-specific political stances in MLLMs. This has practical implications for developers deploying MLLMs in politically sensitive contexts.  The findings highlight that simply building a multilingual model doesn't guarantee that it will reflect the diverse political viewpoints of different linguistic communities.  The work demonstrates the pervasive influence of English data and alignment in shaping opinions even in other languages within a multilingual model.  This provides important insights for future research focused on building more culturally and linguistically aware MLLMs. While focused on Western languages, the findings offer insights applicable to broader cross-lingual bias research. However, the limited scope (Western languages, specific type of alignment) somewhat reduces the overall impact. The analysis would be significantly strengthened by expanding the scope of the examined languages to non-Western languages and by an in-depth qualitative investigation of the content produced by the aligned models. The inclusion of a normative component discussing what constitutes a desirable political stance in MLLMs would further solidify its contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   Rigorous experimental design with robustness checks.
*   Clear articulation of hypotheses and research questions.
*   Use of a well-established political opinion dataset.
*   Demonstration of the impact of English alignment on other languages.
*   Clearly written and well-structured.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited scope (only Western languages considered).
*   The "opinions transfer" finding is somewhat predictable.
*   Lack of a normative discussion on desirable political stances in LLMs.
*   Absence of an in-depth qualitative analysis of the aligned model's content.
* Limited analysis of other alignment datasets that might have been more culturally specific.
* No analysis of the impact of model size on alignment effects.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution by empirically demonstrating the cross-lingual transfer of political opinions in MLLMs and the challenges associated with language-specific political alignment. The work is well-executed and contributes to our understanding of biases in multilingual models. However, the limited scope (Western languages) and predictable findings detract from the novelty and overall significance. Expanding the research to include non-Western languages and explore alternative alignment techniques would significantly improve the impact of the study. Furthermore, the absence of a normative discussion and the limited exploration of alternative alignment datasets constrain its overall contribution. The lack of analysis of the interaction between model size and the alignment effects represents a noticeable gap.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Iterative Learning of Computable Phenotypes for Treatment Resistant Hypertension using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05581v1)
- **Authors**: Guilherme Seidyo Imai Aldeia, Daniel S. Herman, William G. La Cava
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities for medical question answering and programming, but their potential for generating interpretable computable phenotypes (CPs) is under-explored. In this work, we investigate whether LLMs can generate accurate and concise CPs for six clinical phenotypes of varying complexity, which could be leveraged to enable scalable clinical decision support to improve care for patients with hypertension. In addition to evaluating zero-short performance, we propose and test a synthesize, execute, debug, instruct strategy that uses LLMs to generate and iteratively refine CPs using data-driven feedback. Our results show that LLMs, coupled with iterative learning, can generate interpretable and reasonably accurate programs that approach the performance of state-of-the-art ML methods while requiring significantly fewer training examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate interpretable computable phenotypes (CPs) for hypertension-related conditions. It investigates whether LLMs can create accurate and concise CPs, compares them to traditional machine learning (ML) methods, and tests an iterative refinement strategy (SEDI: synthesize, execute, debug, instruct) to improve CP quality. The results suggest that LLMs, especially when combined with the SEDI strategy, can generate reasonably accurate and concise programs, approaching the performance of state-of-the-art ML while requiring fewer training examples. The authors also analyze the impact of prompt richness, feature selection, and model complexity on CP generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel application of LLMs in the context of computable phenotyping. While LLMs have been used in healthcare for tasks like question answering and prediction, the generation of *interpretable* and *executable* phenotypes, followed by iterative refinement using a SEDI strategy, is a significant contribution. The work goes beyond simply using LLMs as black-box predictors and aims to leverage their knowledge for automated model *discovery*.
*   **Significance:** Computable phenotyping is a crucial task in healthcare, but the traditional manual approach is often time-consuming and difficult to scale. The automation of CP generation using LLMs has the potential to accelerate research, improve clinical decision support, and facilitate the adaptation of CPs across different clinical settings. The fact that the LLM-generated CPs are often more interpretable compared to more complex ML models makes this a relevant contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of various LLM settings (different models, prompt richness, feature sets) and the SEDI strategy.
    *   The comparative analysis with traditional ML methods offers valuable insights into the performance of LLMs in this domain.
    *   The discussion on the interpretability of LLM-generated CPs is essential for building trust and facilitating adoption in clinical settings.
    *   The case study on hypertension and its subphenotypes is well-motivated and addresses a significant clinical need.
    *   Acknowledging and discussing potential biases shows awareness and improves rigor.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The results showed LLM-generated CPs underperforming traditional ML in general.
    *   The study is limited to a single dataset from a single healthcare system, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings. The dataset, while having undergone expert review, has been around for a bit and thus lacks novelty itself.
    *   The evaluation of interpretability is somewhat subjective. While the authors argue for the clarity of the generated Python code, a more formal assessment of interpretability could strengthen the paper.
    *   The iterative refinement strategy, while promising, could benefit from more sophisticated feedback mechanisms and automation.
    *   While bias is mentioned, mitigation strategies have been discussed.
    *   The scope, focusing only on hypertension, limits the broader impact and generalizability of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research into the use of LLMs for computable phenotyping and other healthcare applications. The SEDI strategy provides a valuable framework for iteratively refining LLM-generated models using data-driven feedback. The emphasis on interpretability and transparency aligns with the growing demand for trustworthy AI in healthcare.
*   **Areas for Improvement:** Future work could focus on:
    *   Evaluating the approach on diverse datasets and clinical settings.
    *   Developing more formal metrics for assessing the interpretability of generated CPs.
    *   Improving the efficiency and effectiveness of the SEDI strategy through automated feedback and optimization techniques.
    *   Exploring the use of LLMs for generating CPs for a wider range of conditions.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of computable phenotyping by demonstrating the potential of LLMs for automated CP generation and refinement. The comprehensive evaluation and comparative analysis strengthen the findings. While the paper has some limitations in terms of generalizability and scope, the overall contribution is valuable and is likely to influence future research in this area. However, the limitations mentioned, particularly the general underperformance compared to more traditional methods, prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MathSmith: Towards Extremely Hard Mathematical Reasoning by Forging Synthetic Problems with a Reinforced Policy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05592v1)
- **Authors**: Shaoxiong Zhan, Yanlin Lai, Ziyu Lu, Dahua Lin, Ziqing Yang, Fei Tang
- **Abstract**: Large language models have achieved substantial progress in mathematical reasoning, yet their advancement is limited by the scarcity of high-quality, high-difficulty training data. Existing synthesis methods largely rely on transforming human-written templates, limiting both diversity and scalability. We propose MathSmith, a novel framework for synthesizing challenging mathematical problems to enhance LLM reasoning. Rather than modifying existing problems, MathSmith constructs new ones from scratch by randomly sampling concept-explanation pairs from PlanetMath, ensuring data independence and avoiding contamination. To increase difficulty, we design nine predefined strategies as soft constraints during rationales. We further adopts reinforcement learning to jointly optimize structural validity, reasoning complexity, and answer consistency. The length of the reasoning trace generated under autoregressive prompting is used to reflect cognitive complexity, encouraging the creation of more demanding problems aligned with long-chain-of-thought reasoning. Experiments across five benchmarks, categorized as easy & medium (GSM8K, MATH-500) and hard (AIME2024, AIME2025, OlympiadBench), show that MathSmith consistently outperforms existing baselines under both short and long CoT settings. Additionally, a weakness-focused variant generation module enables targeted improvement on specific concepts. Overall, MathSmith exhibits strong scalability, generalization, and transferability, highlighting the promise of high-difficulty synthetic data in advancing LLM reasoning capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MathSmith paper:

**Summary:**

The MathSmith paper introduces a novel framework for synthesizing challenging mathematical problems to improve the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs).  Instead of modifying existing problems or relying on human-written templates, MathSmith constructs new problems from scratch by sampling concept-explanation pairs from PlanetMath. The framework incorporates nine predefined difficulty strategies and uses reinforcement learning to optimize structural validity, reasoning complexity, and answer consistency.  The reasoning trace length generated under autoregressive CoT prompting is used as a proxy for problem difficulty in the reward function. Experiments demonstrate that MathSmith outperforms existing baselines on various benchmarks, particularly on harder problems under long-CoT settings. It also shows that it's effective at improving targeted concepts and generalizes well across different reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper offers several interesting ideas and contributions, but it also has a few limitations that impact its overall novelty and significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty in Generation Approach:** The paper's strength lies in its approach to creating problems from concept-explanation pairs from a comprehensive mathematical knowledge source like PlanetMath, rather than relying on modifying existing problems or human templates. This provides a solid foundation for diversity and independence from existing problem sets.
    *   **Reinforcement Learning for Complexity Control:** The use of reinforcement learning to jointly optimize structural validity, reasoning complexity, and answer consistency is well-motivated and adds a layer of sophistication to the problem generation process. The use of reasoning trace length as a proxy for complexity is also innovative.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The consistent outperformance of MathSmith over existing baselines on multiple benchmarks, especially on hard problems under the long-CoT setting, provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.  The weakness-focused improvement pipeline is also a valuable contribution.
    *   **Scalability and Generalization:** The experiments demonstrating scalability with increasing data volume and model size, and generalization to new reasoning tasks, are compelling.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on GPT-40:**  The initial data generation stage relies on the GPT-4o API for concept summarization and seed data generation.  While GPT-4o is a powerful model, this dependence introduces a potential bias and limitation to the concepts that can be understood and used, limiting the "raw materials" MathSmith can work with.
    *   **Proxy for Complexity:**  While the reasoning trace length is an interesting proxy for complexity, it is not a perfect measure.  A longer trace doesn't *guarantee* deeper reasoning; it could reflect less efficient reasoning or verbosity. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't explore alternative or complementary complexity metrics.
    *   **Olympiad-centric design**: The focus on Olympiad-level questions, while challenging, limits the applicability of the method to other domains of mathematical reasoning. The paper could have broadened the scope and applicability of the method, and made the approach applicable to broader math problems.

*   **Novelty:** The problem synthesis approach based on concept-explanation pairs is the core novelty. The RL component builds on existing techniques but applies them in a unique and useful context, but the lack of focus on mathematical intuition reduces the value.

*   **Significance:** MathSmith represents a step towards more autonomous and scalable methods for generating training data for LLMs in mathematical reasoning.  If it can truly synthesize difficult and original problems, this would significantly impact the field by breaking the dependence on existing problem sets and human annotation.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**.  The paper offers a genuinely novel and promising approach to synthetic data generation. Its use of RL and weakness-focused training is well-executed. However, its dependence on the GPT-4o API for data collection, the proxy metric for complexity, and limited experimental scope constrain its immediate impact on the field. Future work mitigating these limitations could increase its significance substantially.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLaVA-RE: Binary Image-Text Relevancy Evaluation with Multimodal Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05602v1)
- **Authors**: Tao Sun, Oliver Liu, JinJin Li, Lan Ma
- **Abstract**: Multimodal generative AI usually involves generating image or text responses given inputs in another modality. The evaluation of image-text relevancy is essential for measuring response quality or ranking candidate responses. In particular, binary relevancy evaluation, i.e., ``Relevant'' vs. ``Not Relevant'', is a fundamental problem. However, this is a challenging task considering that texts have diverse formats and the definition of relevancy varies in different scenarios. We find that Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are an ideal choice to build such evaluators, as they can flexibly handle complex text formats and take in additional task information. In this paper, we present LLaVA-RE, a first attempt for binary image-text relevancy evaluation with MLLM. It follows the LLaVA architecture and adopts detailed task instructions and multimodal in-context samples. In addition, we propose a novel binary relevancy data set that covers various tasks. Experimental results validate the effectiveness of our framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLaVA-RE, a framework for binary image-text relevancy evaluation using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  LLaVA-RE builds on the LLaVA architecture and incorporates detailed task instructions and multimodal in-context learning to determine if an image and text are "Relevant" or "Not Relevant." The authors also created a novel binary relevancy dataset to train and evaluate their model. They demonstrate that LLaVA-RE outperforms the baseline LLaVA 1.5 model on various tasks, suggesting the effectiveness of their approach for complex image-text relevancy assessment. The key innovations are the architectural adaptations of a pre-existing MLLM, a newly constructed dataset, and specific training methodologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of using MLLMs for binary relevancy evaluation is a reasonable step forward.  Applying LLaVA (a known architecture) to this specific problem is not groundbreaking *per se*, but the authors have made targeted modifications that demonstrate some improvements. The creation of a new dataset specifically for image-text relevancy is valuable because of the lack of available resources. However, the novelty of the methodology itself hinges on the effectiveness and complexity of the data creation process. Using Claude to create instructions and derive negative examples based on semantic similarity is a practical approach, but raises questions about the potential for biases or inaccuracies in the generated data.
*   **Significance:**  Image-text relevancy is a fundamental problem in multimodal AI. Accurate relevancy evaluation is crucial for tasks like retrieval, generation, and dialogue systems. The paper addresses a real-world need, and improvements in this area would have practical benefits. However, the paper's significance is somewhat limited by its incremental nature; it's an application of an existing model with some modifications, rather than a fundamentally new approach. The significance also depends on the dataset.  If the created dataset becomes a widely used benchmark, its impact would be higher.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a practically important problem.
    *   The creation of a new dataset is a useful contribution.
    *   The integration of task instructions and in-context learning are shown to improve performance.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on Claude for instruction generation and negative sampling may introduce bias or inaccuracies into the data. The method could also fail in unexpected edge cases that are not present in training data.
    *   The improvements over the baseline LLaVA model, while present, might not be substantial enough to warrant strong claims of novelty, particularly since it inherits the architecture. A thorough ablation study would be beneficial to highlight the relative contribution of each component (instruction tuning, ICL, dataset).
    *   The paper's discussion of limitations is somewhat shallow and could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the challenges and potential biases in the data and model.
    *   The novelty is limited, applying existing methods to a new dataset.
    *   The choice of binary relevancy constrains the assessment and may not be granular enough for all use cases.

*   **Potential Impact:** The dataset might be the most impactful element of this work if open-sourced and widely adopted. The approach of using an MLLM for this type of task has merit, and the performance gains over the baseline are promising. It is a starting point for more advanced methods, that address the limitations such as inherent biases in datasets and methodologies for producing data, which would be beneficial for the reliability of the model.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:**  The paper provides adequate experimental results to support its claims, however, a more thorough investigation is necessary to determine whether the training method provides a reliable outcome in a multitude of test scenarios, in the absence of task-specific training.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:** The paper presents a worthwhile application of an existing MLLM architecture to a practically relevant problem. The key contribution is the construction of a new dataset and demonstrates moderate performance improvements with architectural adaptations to LLaVA and the introduction of new training methodologies for the model. However, it lacks the novelty and depth required for a truly high-impact contribution. While a useful contribution, the incremental nature of the advancements and the limitations regarding biases in the data creation process, constrain the significance of this work. It could be an important step for creating similar models and methodologies in other areas, however, this is dependent on the development of appropriate training datasets, that do not suffer the same limitations of the LLaVA-RE framework.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Uni-cot: Towards Unified Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Across Text and Vision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05606v1)
- **Authors**: Luozheng Qin, Jia Gong, Yuqing Sun, Tianjiao Li, Mengping Yang, Xiaomeng Yang, Chao Qu, Zhiyu Tan, Hao Li
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has been widely adopted to enhance Large Language Models (LLMs) by decomposing complex tasks into simpler, sequential subtasks. However, extending CoT to vision-language reasoning tasks remains challenging, as it often requires interpreting transitions of visual states to support reasoning. Existing methods often struggle with this due to limited capacity of modeling visual state transitions or incoherent visual trajectories caused by fragmented architectures. To overcome these limitations, we propose Uni-CoT, a Unified Chain-of-Thought framework that enables coherent and grounded multimodal reasoning within a single unified model. The key idea is to leverage a model capable of both image understanding and generation to reason over visual content and model evolving visual states. However, empowering a unified model to achieve that is non-trivial, given the high computational cost and the burden of training. To address this, Uni-CoT introduces a novel two-level reasoning paradigm: A Macro-Level CoT for high-level task planning and A Micro-Level CoT for subtask execution. This design significantly reduces the computational overhead. Furthermore, we introduce a structured training paradigm that combines interleaved image-text supervision for macro-level CoT with multi-task objectives for micro-level CoT. Together, these innovations allow Uni-CoT to perform scalable and coherent multi-modal reasoning. Furthermore, thanks to our design, all experiments can be efficiently completed using only 8 A100 GPUs with 80GB VRAM each. Experimental results on reasoning-driven image generation benchmark (WISE) and editing benchmarks (RISE and KRIS) indicates that Uni-CoT demonstrates SOTA performance and strong generalization, establishing Uni-CoT as a promising solution for multi-modal reasoning. Project Page and Code: https://sais-fuxi.github.io/projects/uni-cot/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Uni-CoT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Uni-CoT, a unified Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework designed to enhance vision-language reasoning in large language models (LLMs).  Uni-CoT aims to overcome limitations in existing approaches related to modeling dynamic visual state transitions. It does this through a two-level reasoning paradigm: a Macro-Level CoT for high-level task planning and a Micro-Level CoT for subtask execution. The framework utilizes a single, unified model (BAGEL) capable of both image understanding and generation. The authors address computational and training complexity issues through hierarchical reasoning, interleaved image-text supervision, and multi-task auxiliary objectives.  Experiments on reasoning-driven image generation and editing benchmarks (WISE, RISE, KRIS) demonstrate state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of a unified model for both understanding and generating visual content within a CoT framework has merit. The hierarchical Macro/Micro-CoT structure is a reasonable approach to manage complexity and reduce computational cost. The structured training regime combining interleaved and auxiliary losses seems sensible, and the masking strategy to enforce abstractions appears useful.

    However, the incremental advance over existing CoT methods for vision-language tasks must be rigorously scrutinized. While the paper claims advantages in visual state transition modeling, it's essential to assess the degree to which this is genuinely novel and superior. The reliance on BAGEL, while understandable, could limit the novelty if the improvements are tightly coupled to that specific architecture. The architectural choices for Macro- and Micro-CoT, such as the MDP formulation, are not revolutionary by themselves but represent clever engineering.

*   **Significance:** If the performance gains are substantiated, and if Uni-CoT demonstrates a genuine improvement in coherence and grounding of visual reasoning, the work could have a tangible impact. The interpretable, step-by-step reasoning paradigm is valuable for debugging and understanding the model's behavior. Scalability is critical, and demonstrating good performance on 8 A100 GPUs is a positive indicator.

    However, the significance hinges on demonstrating convincingly that Uni-CoT addresses fundamental limitations in existing vision-language reasoning methods. The experimental results on WISE, RISE, and KRIS are promising, but more detailed ablation studies are needed to isolate the benefits of each component (Macro-CoT, Micro-CoT, masking, and training paradigm). The paper also needs to address potential weaknesses. Are there specific types of visual reasoning problems where Uni-CoT struggles? How does it compare to other complex VLM architectures (e.g., those that employ external memory or knowledge retrieval)? Does the hierarchical design limit learning long-range dependencies?

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a relevant problem: Coherent vision-language reasoning.
    *   Proposes a well-structured framework with Macro/Micro-CoT.
    *   Leverages a unified model for understanding and generation.
    *   Employs a reasonable training paradigm.
    *   Achieves state-of-the-art results on several benchmarks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Incremental novelty, heavily reliant on BAGEL.
    *   Requires a more in-depth ablation study.
    *   Limited discussion of failure cases and limitations.
    *   Lack of comprehensive comparison to other advanced VLM architectures.

**Justification of Score:**

Based on the critical evaluation above, a score of 7 reflects a solid contribution but highlights areas where further validation and analysis are needed.

*   The paper presents a well-engineered system that achieves good performance.
*   The core ideas (unified model, hierarchical CoT) are reasonable, although not revolutionary, and provide a practical approach to a challenging problem.
*   However, the reliance on BAGEL and the need for deeper ablation studies slightly limit its novelty and potential impact. The lack of detailed failure analysis and comprehensive comparison to other VLMs also impact score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Shuffle-R1: Efficient RL framework for Multimodal Large Language Models via Data-centric Dynamic Shuffle
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05612v1)
- **Authors**: Linghao Zhu, Yiran Guan, Dingkang Liang, Jianzhong Ju, Zhenbo Luo, Bin Qin, Jian Luan, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Bai
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as an effective post-training paradigm for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language model (MLLM). However, current RL pipelines often suffer from training inefficiencies caused by two underexplored issues: Advantage Collapsing, where most advantages in a batch concentrate near zero, and Rollout Silencing, where the proportion of rollouts contributing non-zero gradients diminishes over time. These issues lead to suboptimal gradient updates and hinder long-term learning efficiency. To address these issues, we propose Shuffle-R1, a simple yet principled framework that improves RL fine-tuning efficiency by dynamically restructuring trajectory sampling and batch composition. It introduces (1) Pairwise Trajectory Sampling, which selects high-contrast trajectories with large advantages to improve gradient signal quality, and (2) Advantage-based Trajectory Shuffle, which increases exposure of valuable rollouts through informed batch reshuffling. Experiments across multiple reasoning benchmarks show that our framework consistently outperforms strong RL baselines with minimal overhead. These results highlight the importance of data-centric adaptations for more efficient RL training in MLLM.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the Shuffle-R1 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Shuffle-R1, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the training efficiency of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The framework addresses two underexplored issues that hinder current RL pipelines: "Advantage Collapsing," where most advantages in a batch are near zero, and "Rollout Silencing," where the proportion of contributing rollouts with non-zero gradients decreases over time. Shuffle-R1 employs two key modules to combat these issues: (1) Pairwise Trajectory Sampling (PTS), which selects high-contrast trajectory pairs with large-magnitude advantages, and (2) Advantage-based Batch Shuffle (ABS), which dynamically reshapes training batches to prioritize informative trajectories and alleviate Rollout Silencing. Experiments demonstrate that Shuffle-R1 outperforms strong RL baselines on multiple reasoning benchmarks with minimal computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in identifying and addressing two critical inefficiencies in RL fine-tuning for MLLMs: Advantage Collapsing and Rollout Silencing. While RL fine-tuning and data-centric approaches are not entirely new, the specific combination of these ideas with the proposed PTS and ABS modules offers a novel solution. The adaptive sampling and batch composition are also valuable contributions.
*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by showing how data-centric adaptations can improve the efficiency of RL training in MLLMs. By addressing the identified inefficiencies, the framework enables faster convergence, better data utilization, and improved model performance on reasoning tasks.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problems:** The paper clearly articulates the Advantage Collapsing and Rollout Silencing problems, making the motivation for the proposed solution easy to understand.
    *   **Principled Approach:** The PTS and ABS modules are based on sound theoretical principles and are well-motivated.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental setup is well-designed and includes a wide range of reasoning benchmarks and model scales. The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of Shuffle-R1.
    *   **Competitive Results:** The framework achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing strong RL baselines and even outperforming closed-source models like GPT-40 and Claude-3.7 on some benchmarks.
    *   **Efficiency:** Shuffle-R1 improves training efficiency, achieving comparable results to GRPO with only half the training steps.
    *   **Modular Design:** The modular design of Shuffle-R1 allows for seamless integration with existing RL algorithms.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the proposed framework achieves significant performance gains, the underlying techniques are built upon existing RL algorithms.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of Shuffle-R1 may be sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters, such as the sampling ratio α and the shuffle times S. The paper provides some guidance on setting these parameters but a more thorough investigation of their impact would be beneficial.
    *   **Scope:** The focus is primarily on MLLMs for reasoning tasks. The framework's applicability to other types of language models or different domains could be further explored.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of RL for MLLMs by highlighting the importance of data-centric adaptations and providing a practical framework for improving training efficiency. The ideas presented in the paper could inspire further research on adaptive sampling techniques, batch composition strategies, and other data-centric methods for RL.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 is justified. The identification and addressing of Advantage Collapsing and Rollout Silencing are crucial contributions, and the effectiveness of Shuffle-R1 is convincingly demonstrated through extensive experiments. While the framework builds on existing RL techniques and may be sensitive to hyperparameter settings, the overall impact on the field is potentially substantial, especially for those working on RL fine-tuning for MLLMs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cooper: Co-Optimizing Policy and Reward Models in Reinforcement Learning for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05613v1)
- **Authors**: Haitao Hong, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Guiyang Hou, Wenqi Zhang, Weiming Lu, Yongliang Shen, Jun Xiao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in reasoning tasks, where reinforcement learning (RL) serves as a key algorithm for enhancing their reasoning capabilities. Currently, there are two mainstream reward paradigms: model-based rewards and rule-based rewards. However, both approaches suffer from limitations: rule-based rewards lack robustness, while model-based rewards are vulnerable to reward hacking. To address these issues, we propose Cooper(Co-optimizing Policy Model and Reward Model), a RL framework that jointly optimizes both the policy model and the reward model. Cooper leverages the high precision of rule-based rewards when identifying correct responses, and dynamically constructs and selects positive-negative sample pairs for continued training the reward model. This design enhances robustness and mitigates the risk of reward hacking. To further support Cooper, we introduce a hybrid annotation strategy that efficiently and accurately generates training data for the reward model. We also propose a reference-based reward modeling paradigm, where the reward model takes a reference answer as input. Based on this design, we train a reward model named VerifyRM, which achieves higher accuracy on VerifyBench compared to other models of the same size. We conduct reinforcement learning using both VerifyRM and Cooper. Our experiments show that Cooper not only alleviates reward hacking but also improves end-to-end RL performance, for instance, achieving a 0.54% gain in average accuracy on Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct. Our findings demonstrate that dynamically updating reward model is an effective way to combat reward hacking, providing a reference for better integrating reward models into RL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "COOPER: CO-OPTIMIZING POLICY AND REWARD MODELS IN REINFORCEMENT LEARNING FOR LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Cooper, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework for large language models (LLMs) that aims to overcome the limitations of existing reward paradigms.  Specifically, it addresses the issues of robustness in rule-based rewards and reward hacking in model-based rewards. Cooper co-optimizes both the policy model and the reward model. It leverages high-precision rule-based rewards to identify correct responses and then dynamically constructs positive and negative sample pairs to continuously train the reward model. This is designed to enhance robustness and mitigate reward hacking. The paper also presents a hybrid annotation strategy for generating reward model training data and a reference-based reward modeling paradigm called VerifyRM. Experiments demonstrate that Cooper alleviates reward hacking and improves end-to-end RL performance compared to using either rule-based rewards or fixed reward models alone.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of co-optimizing policy and reward models in RL for LLMs is somewhat novel. While the concept of iterative refinement or online adaptation is not entirely new in machine learning, its specific application to jointly training policy and reward models to mitigate reward hacking in the LLM domain is a valuable contribution. The hybrid annotation strategy and the reference-based reward model, VerifyRM, are also incrementally novel. There are other methods that combine rule-based and model-based approaches, but the specific mechanism Cooper employs to dynamically update reward model parameters during RL, focusing on improving the reward model on examples where the rule-based verifier is high-precision, is a differentiating factor.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and important problem in applying RL to LLMs: reward hacking. The demonstrated improvements in performance compared to standard RL approaches, particularly in complex reasoning tasks, highlight the significance of the proposed framework. Furthermore, the detailed analysis of training dynamics and reward model stability provides valuable insights into the behavior of such systems and offers guidance for future research. The VerifyRM achieves state of the art performance on VerifyBench, further enhancing its significance. By empirically showing the problem of reward hacking with fixed reward models, the paper makes a strong case for dynamic reward model updates.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing reward paradigms for RL in LLMs.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** Cooper provides a well-defined and implementable framework for co-optimizing policy and reward models.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are comprehensive and demonstrate the effectiveness of Cooper across various benchmarks. The baseline comparisons are appropriate.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of training dynamics and reward model stability provides valuable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Rule-Based Rewards:** The reliance on high-precision rule-based rewards for positive sample selection may limit the applicability of Cooper to domains where such rewards are not readily available. However, the authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest exploring self-supervised contrastive example generation as a future direction.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** Dual optimization may lead to increased computational demands, affecting scalability.
    *   **Assistant LLM dependency:** Reliance on an assistant LLM to generate negative samples introduces another external dependency. While this can be a weakness, the authors mention the development of self-supervised contrastive example generation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the way RL is applied to LLMs, particularly in tasks where reward hacking is a concern. It encourages researchers to consider dynamic reward model updates and offers a concrete framework for doing so. The hybrid annotation strategy and VerifyRM can also be valuable resources for the community.

*   **Justification of Score:**  While the paper's contributions are valuable and the experimental results are convincing, the heavy reliance on domain-specific rule based reward and auxiliary models detract from the overall novelty and generalizability. The core concept of co-optimizing policy and reward model itself is already done in other domains in the past. Therefore, a score of 7 represents a solid contribution with practical value but acknowledges limitations in terms of broad applicability and fundamental novelty.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### OmniEAR: Benchmarking Agent Reasoning in Embodied Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05614v1)
- **Authors**: Zixuan Wang, Dingming Li, Hongxing Li, Shuo Chen, Yuchen Yan, Wenqi Zhang, Yongliang Shen, Weiming Lu, Jun Xiao, Yueting Zhuang
- **Abstract**: Large language models excel at abstract reasoning but their capacity for embodied agent reasoning remains largely unexplored. We present OmniEAR, a comprehensive framework for evaluating how language models reason about physical interactions, tool usage, and multi-agent coordination in embodied tasks. Unlike existing benchmarks that provide predefined tool sets or explicit collaboration directives, OmniEAR requires agents to dynamically acquire capabilities and autonomously determine coordination strategies based on task demands. Through text-based environment representation, we model continuous physical properties and complex spatial relationships across 1,500 scenarios spanning household and industrial domains. Our systematic evaluation reveals severe performance degradation when models must reason from constraints: while achieving 85-96% success with explicit instructions, performance drops to 56-85% for tool reasoning and 63-85% for implicit collaboration, with compound tasks showing over 50% failure rates. Surprisingly, complete environmental information degrades coordination performance, indicating models cannot filter task-relevant constraints. Fine-tuning improves single-agent tasks dramatically (0.6% to 76.3%) but yields minimal multi-agent gains (1.5% to 5.5%), exposing fundamental architectural limitations. These findings demonstrate that embodied reasoning poses fundamentally different challenges than current models can address, establishing OmniEAR as a rigorous benchmark for evaluating and advancing embodied AI systems. Our code and data are included in the supplementary materials and will be open-sourced upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OMNIEAR: BENCHMARKING AGENT REASONING IN EMBODIED TASKS":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniEAR, a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning abilities of embodied agents, particularly Large Language Models (LLMs), in simulated environments.  OmniEAR focuses on evaluating how well agents can reason about physical properties, use tools to overcome capability limitations, and autonomously coordinate with other agents, all driven by the demands of the task and the environment. Unlike existing benchmarks that offer predefined tools or explicit collaboration instructions, OmniEAR requires agents to dynamically acquire capabilities and determine coordination strategies. The authors use a text-based environment representation and evaluate models across a wide range of scenarios in household and industrial domains, demonstrating significant performance degradation when agents need to reason from constraints rather than follow explicit instructions. The paper also shows limitations in current models related to handling environmental information, scaling to multi-agent scenarios, and grounding abstract concepts in physical properties.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a significant step forward in embodied reasoning benchmarks.  Existing benchmarks often simplify the physical reasoning component, whereas OmniEAR forces agents to reason dynamically about physical attributes like weight and material, to identify when tools are needed and when collaboration is essential. The emphasis on *implicit* collaboration, driven by physical constraints rather than explicit instructions, is a crucial and innovative aspect.

**Significance:** The identified performance gaps expose critical weaknesses in current LLMs' ability to truly understand and interact with embodied environments. The benchmark clearly shows that while LLMs can excel at abstract reasoning or following explicit instructions, their performance drops significantly when physical constraints and autonomous decision-making are involved. By highlighting these limitations, the paper paves the way for developing AI systems that are better grounded in the physical world. It presents a more realistic and challenging assessment of agent reasoning capabilities compared to existing methodologies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Focus on embodied reasoning fundamentals:** The paper correctly identifies and targets key aspects of embodied intelligence, like reasoning about physical properties, tool usage, and autonomous collaboration.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The framework includes a large number of diverse scenarios with various task complexities and agent configurations.
*   **Clear identification of limitations:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence of current LLMs' limitations in embodied reasoning, along with valuable insights into their potential causes (e.g., inability to filter irrelevant information, poor grounding of physical constraints).
*   **Well-defined framework:** OmniEAR is presented as a well-structured framework with detailed explanations about the environment representation, task formalization, and benchmark generation.
*   **Analysis of scaling behavior:** The investigation of how model scale and reasoning-specialized architectures affect performance adds to the paper's value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Text-based abstraction:** While the text-based representation allows for scalability, it abstracts away complexities of continuous control, sensorimotor feedback, and real-time physical interactions, which are important components of real-world embodiment. Future work should explore how these abstracted components affect the performance.
*   **Reliance on centralized coordination:** The evaluation primarily uses centralized coordination in multi-agent scenarios, which might not fully capture the challenges of decentralized collaboration with communication limitations.

**Potential Influence:**

OmniEAR has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in embodied AI.  By providing a rigorous and challenging benchmark, it can drive the development of new architectures and training methods that address the identified limitations. The focus on implicit collaboration could also spur more research on designing agents capable of autonomous decision-making in complex, multi-agent environments.

**Justification for Score:**

While the text-based representation is a valid choice for evaluation, the abstraction it generates limits the score to a 7.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TrajEvo: Trajectory Prediction Heuristics Design via LLM-driven Evolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05616v1)
- **Authors**: Zhikai Zhao, Chuanbo Hua, Federico Berto, Kanghoon Lee, Zihan Ma, Jiachen Li, Jinkyoo Park
- **Abstract**: Trajectory prediction is a critical task in modeling human behavior, especially in safety-critical domains such as social robotics and autonomous vehicle navigation. Traditional heuristics based on handcrafted rules often lack accuracy and generalizability. Although deep learning approaches offer improved performance, they typically suffer from high computational cost, limited explainability, and, importantly, poor generalization to out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. In this paper, we introduce TrajEvo, a framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically design trajectory prediction heuristics. TrajEvo employs an evolutionary algorithm to generate and refine prediction heuristics from past trajectory data. We propose two key innovations: Cross-Generation Elite Sampling to encourage population diversity, and a Statistics Feedback Loop that enables the LLM to analyze and improve alternative predictions. Our evaluations demonstrate that TrajEvo outperforms existing heuristic methods across multiple real-world datasets, and notably surpasses both heuristic and deep learning methods in generalizing to an unseen OOD real-world dataset. TrajEvo marks a promising step toward the automated design of fast, explainable, and generalizable trajectory prediction heuristics. We release our source code to facilitate future research at https://github.com/ai4co/trajevo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRAJEVO, a novel framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) and evolutionary algorithms (EAs) to automate the design of trajectory prediction heuristics.  The framework aims to address the limitations of traditional hand-crafted heuristics (lack of accuracy and generalizability) and deep learning approaches (high computational cost, limited explainability, and poor out-of-distribution performance).  TRAJEVO iteratively generates, evaluates, and refines trajectory prediction heuristics using LLMs, incorporating two key innovations: Cross-Generation Elite Sampling (CGES) to maintain population diversity and a Statistics Feedback Loop (SFL) to enable the LLM to analyze and improve predictions. The paper demonstrates that TRAJEVO outperforms existing heuristic methods and, importantly, shows improved generalization to unseen, out-of-distribution datasets compared to both heuristics and deep learning methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs within an evolutionary loop to automatically design *trajectory prediction heuristics* is novel.  While there has been prior work combining LLMs with EAs for algorithm design, applying it specifically to trajectory prediction with a focus on efficiency, interpretability, and out-of-distribution generalization is a significant contribution. The introduction of CGES and SFL are also novel contributions specifically tailored for this application.
* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical problem in robotics and autonomous systems: achieving robust and reliable trajectory prediction, particularly in safety-critical domains. TRAJEVO provides a promising alternative to complex deep learning models, offering a way to design fast, explainable, and generalizable heuristics.  The OOD performance results are particularly significant, as they demonstrate TRAJEVO's potential for deployment in real-world scenarios where adaptability to unseen environments is essential. The focus on computational efficiency is also very important for robotics applications with limited resources.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel framework:** TRAJEVO introduces a new and potentially powerful approach to trajectory prediction heuristic design.
    * **Strong OOD performance:** The experimental results clearly demonstrate superior out-of-distribution generalization compared to both heuristic and deep learning baselines.
    * **Emphasis on efficiency and interpretability:** The framework generates computationally efficient and human-readable heuristics, which is crucial for many applications.
    * **Ablation Study:** The ablation study provides evidence for the effectiveness of the CGES and SFL components.
    * **LLM Stability and Performance Evaluations** Evaluating the performance when using a variety of LLMs indicates a robustness in the proposed framework.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **In-Distribution Performance Gap:** The paper acknowledges that TRAJEVO's in-distribution performance is not as high as the most specialized deep learning models. While the OOD gains are impressive, further bridging this in-distribution gap would strengthen the argument.
    * **Limited Input Data Complexity** Current experiments only make use of positional data, which is a simplification over what could be included and integrated within the model, such as agent types, perceptions, or map info.
    * **Downstream Task Performance: **The work leverages the typical trajectory perdition metrics. However, these might not always correlate directly into the task at hand, and should be addressed for a more specific and direct application.

* **Potential Influence:** TRAJEVO has the potential to significantly influence the field of trajectory prediction, particularly in areas where computational efficiency, interpretability, and OOD robustness are paramount. It could inspire new research into automated heuristic design and the integration of LLMs into robotics and autonomous systems.  The work highlights the importance of designing algorithms that can generalize to unseen environments, a crucial aspect for real-world deployment.
* **Clarity and Presentation**: The paper is generally well-written and presents the concepts clearly. The figures and tables are helpful for understanding the framework and results.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field. While the in-distribution performance is not state-of-the-art, the novel framework, the strong OOD performance, and the emphasis on efficiency and interpretability make it a valuable contribution. The work provides a promising alternative approach to trajectory prediction, addressing limitations of existing methods. Further investigation into enhancing the in-distribution performance and exploration of new and complex input datasets has potential to raise the score.
